Haypp · HAYPP-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Haypp

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 49,450 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces are a strong, structural, multi-year tailwind for Haypp's revenue, and a weaker and more fragile support for its profit. The single most important external fact is channel migration: online is about 3% of US pouch sales against 36% in Sweden, migration is driven by a mechanical usage threshold, and Haypp owns roughly four-fifths of the online channel in its markets. Industry drivers (category innovation, state taxes, manufacturer online-first launches) are currently accelerating that migration. Against this, three forces cap confidence: (1) the reported 2026 growth rate is flattered by lapping the ZYN absence and by Core price and excise pass-through, so the second-set model should step growth down in 2027; (2) the manufacturers that drive volume also control supply and, through Media and Insights, much of Haypp's gross profit; (3) regulation and the delivery chain carry fat left tails, and the current market regime penalises small, illiquid growth stocks. Net: external forces support a base case of roughly 16 to 20% revenue growth a year in 2027 and 2028 after about 25% in 2026, with a low-probability but severe downside branch that the model should carry explicitly as a scenario.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0258C-0555C-0752C-0945C-3560C-3948
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B584th of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity among online retailers is stable to falling, which is good for Haypp; competitive intensity among the manufacturers that supply it is rising sharply, which is mixed: it lowers the price per pouch and squeezes Growth price/mix, but it also raises the trade-marketing budgets the manufacturers spend on Haypp's Media and Insights.

Reasoning:

  • Retail level (Haypp's direct rivals): weak and weakening. Haypp is about 14 times its nearest online rival (Deutsche Bank) and about 10 times Prilla, its main US online competitor. The CEO says roughly 60 online challengers have failed over his tenure. New UK entrants (Snus Vikings, Two Wombats) rely on paid social media, which the UK advertising and sponsorship ban from 1 June 2027 will close; UK compliance rules (age 18 from October 2026, free-sample ban) raise their costs. Manufacturer-owned online sites are about 1% of online volumes.
  • Manufacturer level: intensifying. BAT's Velo took US volume share from 16.2% (2025) to about 28 to 30% (H1 2026) with heavy promotions; ZYN volumes fell 1.2% in October 2026; Altria's on! cut promotions and raised price 21.6% to $4.90 a can; Imperial bought Helwit (SEK 515m, September 2026); KT&G plans pouch entry into about 70 countries by 2030; cheaper Chinese imports are entering Europe. US competitor retail prices fell 12% year on year on promotions (Altria, late 2025).
  • Transmission to Haypp. Promotions cut Growth gross margin to 18.9% from 24.4% and Growth average order value 8% to SEK 785 (Q2 2026); Growth price/mix was negative. But the promotions are manufacturer-funded and partly offset in cost of goods sold; and a crowded manufacturer field is exactly when brand owners pay most for online visibility, launches (ZYN Ultra online-first on Haypp, 15 June 2026; STG's XQS tested online first) and data. Media and Insights grew about 38 to 41% year on year in 2026.
  • The asymmetric danger. A manufacturer that loses share may choose to cut supply or terms to the online channel (PMI's 2024 pause is the precedent), and consolidation among brand owners would shrink Media and Insights budgets.

For the model: no new retail competitor material enough to cap Haypp's share in 18 months; keep a negative Growth price/mix in 2026 to mid-2027, offset by Media and Insights growth above product growth.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

B+553rd of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: near-term the net effect is a mild tailwind (supply has normalised, manufacturer price rises pass straight through and lift revenue, and manufacturer competition is funding promotions and Media and Insights), but supplier concentration is extreme and unchanged, so the structural exposure remains Haypp's single largest business risk.

Reasoning:

  • Availability: normalised. PMI resumed ZYN online in the US on 11 September 2025 under "a mutually beneficial agreement that utilized Media and Insights services", and invested about USD 600m in a new Colorado plant and expanded Owensboro, Kentucky to about 900 million cans; supply-side bottlenecks should not recur from capacity.
  • Cost of goods: pass-through. About 82% of group expenses are goods for resale, bought in local currency with fast turnover (inventory turns about 11 times; inventory SEK 315m at Q3 2026). Haypp does not lock inbound prices for the year and re-prices dynamically; it built stock ahead of manufacturer price rises in late 2025 and earned a higher gross margin on the sell-through. Price rises inflate revenue at constant percentage margins, so they are a revenue tailwind.
  • Concentration: extreme. The top three manufacturers hold about 87% of pouch volume; PMI, BAT and Altria own the dominant brands. Brand owners earn about 15% more selling through Haypp than through physical retail, which aligns incentives, but PMI's 2024 pause proved a single supplier decision can remove a brand worth 46% of US sales for over a year.
  • The Media and Insights entanglement. Because a large part of Haypp's gross profit is paid by those same suppliers, concentration cuts both ways: suppliers fund the profit and can withdraw it. I judge this a balanced but fragile equilibrium.

For the model: gross margin can be held at about 19.5 to 20% with upside from Media and Insights mix; treat a supplier dispute as a scenario, not a base-case input.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

A521st of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro forces act weakly on Haypp's demand (which is staple-like and, if anything, counter-cyclical because tighter budgets push users to cheaper online buying) and most strongly on its valuation and reported earnings, through the discount rate for a small-cap growth stock in a risk-off regime and through unhedged currency translation.

Reasoning:

  • Demand level: little bite. Nicotine demand is price-inelastic; management and DNB Carnegie both note that budget pressure drives traffic online where prices are 20 to 50% lower. Swedish online nicotine outgrew e-commerce in a weak September 2026 retail month. The Swedish average nicotine e-commerce basket fell to SEK 740 from SEK 810 in Q3 2026 (Swedish Retail Federation), a modest sign of basket trading-down.
  • Earnings level: currency. About half of sales and costs are non-SEK (2025: SEK 48%, NOK 22%, USD 20%, GBP 7%, EUR 3%); no derivatives are used. Transaction exposure is small (local purchasing, fast turns), but translation is material: reported 2025 growth was 4.6% against 7% organic. More important for EPS, intercompany loan revaluation runs through net financial items: net finance costs were SEK 12.5m in 2025 (SEK 5.2m in 2024) and SEK 9.4m in Q3 2026 alone, and Deutsche Bank attributes its FY2026 EPS cut entirely to this. Nordea forecasts USD/SEK peaking at 10.32 in mid-2027 and falling to 9.29 by end-2028, a translation headwind in 2028 when the Growth (USD) segment should become profitable. Note that a stronger SEK against USD reduces Growth losses in SEK while the segment is loss-making, and reduces profits once it is profitable.
  • Valuation level: the hardest bite. The Portfolio Fitness for Fighting digest (6 November 2026) reads the regime as risk-off beneath an index high, with Fragility (small and less simple stocks) at Double Underweight and Momentum Underweight; Consumer staples and Tobacco are Overweight, but the market is "choosing its defensives". Haypp is a SEK 4.9bn, illiquid (about USD 0.5m traded a day) growth stock at the 83rd to 87th percentile of its own multiple history; that is the profile this regime sells. Many Nordic funds cannot own tobacco-related shares.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–45

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other external dynamics matter, two negative and one positive: (negative) investor-base constraints and illiquidity that cap the multiple and amplify price moves; (negative) the last-mile delivery chain as an unpriced single point of failure; (positive) state fiscal dependence on legal pouch sales, which gives governments a reason to police illicit sellers rather than ban the category.

Reasoning:

  • Investor base. Many Nordic institutions cannot hold tobacco-related equities; DNB Carnegie dropped coverage citing limited trading volumes; average daily value traded is about USD 0.4 to 0.5m; there is no listed option market. The share price is therefore set by a small holder base (founders and venture funds GR8 Ventures 12.62%, Patrik Rees 11.68%, Northerner Holding 9.63%; Fidelity 9.87%; Robotti 5.41%), which means moves on news are large in both directions (late-2024 and late-2025 drawdowns took the forward P/E to about 10x).
  • Last-mile delivery. A former Reynolds American director calls carrier restrictions on shipping pouches the single largest existential risk; parcel-locker age rules in Sweden show how delivery rules can change by court ruling. Haypp's mitigation is technology (biometric verification, multi-carrier shipping software), not contract. I judge the probability low in 18 months but the impact severe.
  • Fiscal capture. As states tax pouches, they gain revenue and a reason to enforce age limits and remove unregulated imports (illicit products hold about 15% of the US pouch market). That supports compliant, scaled sellers.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B605th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: standard entrants (new online pouch shops) are a negligible threat; adjacent entrants (manufacturers selling direct, generalist marketplaces) are a low near-term but medium long-term threat; orthogonal entrants (quick-commerce delivery apps and AI shopping agents) are the least discussed and, in my judgement, the most credible medium-term threat to Haypp's share of incremental online demand.

By type:

  • Standard entrants: about 60 have failed; UK newcomers depend on paid social media soon to be banned; Haypp has a 14-times scale lead, superior procurement terms and SEO authority. Threat: low.
  • Adjacent, manufacturers direct: about 1% of online volume; single-brand sites fail the variety test; US multi-state tax and flavour compliance is costly; but a senior PMI brand manager argues brand owners should own consumer relationships, and automated tax-registry software could lower the barrier. Threat: low within 18 months, medium over three to five years, highest if one maker becomes dominant enough not to need a neutral retailer.
  • Adjacent, generalist marketplaces (Amazon and similar): kept out by age verification and adult-signature requirements that break one-click checkout, plus reputational risk. Threat: low.
  • Adjacent, Asian and new manufacturers (KT&G, Chinese imports): these widen Haypp's assortment rather than compete with it.
  • Orthogonal, quick-commerce (my hypothesis; not covered in the files): US delivery apps already deliver age-restricted goods in under an hour; they would attack the immediacy gap that keeps impulse users in stores. They do not have Haypp's range or price, so they would compete for the light user rather than the heavy user Haypp serves best. Worth targeted research.
  • Orthogonal, AI agents: an AI shopping agent does not feel loyalty (Wisdom Library: "The buying agent does not feel loyalty") and could route repeat purchases to the cheapest compliant seller; Haypp's price leadership is a defence, but its SEO advantage would matter less.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4814th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are currently a support, actively so: they launch products online first through Haypp, fund promotions, buy its data and advertising, and negotiated "mutually beneficial" terms for 2026. They remain the single largest source of risk, because three of them control almost all volume, one has already withdrawn its leading brand from Haypp's US platforms for over a year, and they pay a large part of Haypp's gross profit, which gives them leverage over Haypp's profits as well as its volumes.

Reasoning:

  • Support: PMI launched moist ZYN Ultra online first on Nicokick and Northerner (15 June 2026); STG tests XQS flavours online first; brand owners earn about 15% better margins online and reinvest some of it in promotions on Haypp; annual commercial negotiations for 2026 concluded on "supportive", "highly constructive" terms (late 2025).
  • Risk: PMI's 2024 to 2025 online pause (ZYN was 46% of US sales); BAT's Velo now about 29% of US volume, so Haypp's dependence is spreading across two makers rather than one, which is a modest improvement; manufacturers could restrict online discount margins or prioritise convenience stores (Deutsche Bank).
  • Mitigants: the indispensable-retailer argument (a former BAT global head likens delisting from Haypp to a brand leaving Walmart); a range of 3,500 SKUs; the direct PMI procurement contract; Media and Insights as an embedded service.
  • For the model: gross margin and Media and Insights growth are, in effect, outputs of the supplier relationship; a supplier-terms sensitivity (for example, a 1 percentage point lower gross margin, about SEK 58m of EBITDA in 2027, roughly 17% of consensus EBITDA) belongs in the second set.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0178C-0368C-0448C-0878C-3276C-3750C-4380
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

A781st of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand for Haypp's channel is changing structurally and strongly in its favour, with unusually high multi-year visibility; but the reported 2026 growth rate overstates the underlying structural rate, because 2026 laps the absence of ZYN in the US and benefits from one-off price and excise pass-through in Core.

Reasoning:

  • The structural force is channel migration, not category growth alone. Online is about 36% of pouch sales in Sweden against about 3% in the US and about 7% in the UK (Deutsche Bank, April 2026). Users move online once consumption reaches seven to eight pouches a day (Jefferies, July 2026); the average US user is at about 3.6 to 4 pouches a day against 10 to 12 in Scandinavia. As US users' daily consumption rises (existing users report rising usage; 40% already use eight or more a day), the share of users above the online threshold rises mechanically. This is a slow, compounding, non-cyclical driver with years of runway: even a move from 3% to 10% US online share, far short of Sweden's 36%, triples the online pool.
  • Category growth stacks on top. The global pouch market was about USD 11.2bn retail value in 2024, forecast at about 21% value CAGR to 2029 (Euromonitor via Deutsche Bank); the US is 78% of value. UK pouches grew 55% year on year to about £230m; Haypp's UK pouch volumes grew 93% in Q3 2026.
  • Demand is staple-like. Returning customers are more than 90% of net sales; return rates are below 0.4%; seasonality is limited to July and December holiday phasing. Swedish online nicotine sales grew 6% in January to September 2026 while Swedish e-commerce grew 2%.
  • What flatters 2026. Haypp lost ZYN in the US from mid-2024 until 11 September 2025 (ZYN had been 46% of US sales in Q2 2024). Growth-segment revenue fell 26% in Q2 2025. The 2026 quarters (+24%, +27%, +26% at constant currency) therefore compare against a ZYN-less base until Q3; from Q4 2026 the comparison includes ZYN. Core's +19% in Q3 2026 (15.7% organic) includes manufacturer price increases passed through and the Swedish weight-based pouch excise of SEK 209 per kilogram from January 2026; Core pouch volumes grew only 14%, snus 1%, Core active consumers 6.4%.
  • What dilutes measured demand. ZYN's move to 20-pouch cans (from 15) in Q4 2026 cuts revenue per pouch by about 25% at a steady can price; customers buy fewer cans for the same pouch consumption, so revenue in cans grows more slowly than pouch use.

For the model: treat the structural channel-plus-category growth for the Growth segment as roughly 30 to 40% a year for 2027 (down from about 50% in 2026 once the ZYN base effect ends) and Core as high single digits once 2026 price and excise effects annualise, with low cyclicality. Visibility is high on direction (multi-year), medium on the rate.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

A681st of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the main disruption force, the migration of nicotine from combustibles and from physical retail to pouches bought online, is strengthening and acts for Haypp; the two disruption forces that act against it (manufacturer direct-to-consumer selling and AI-driven search changing how customers find retailers) are not yet materialising, but the second is the more credible medium-term threat to the 97% organic-traffic model.

Reasoning:

  • For Haypp (strengthening). Pouches offer 50 to 65% savings against cigarettes; US FDA granted modified-risk status to 20 ZYN variants (June 2026), authorised ZYN Ultra variants (August 2026) and adopted enforcement discretion for pending applications (May 2026), lowering the federal risk premium on the category. Physical retail backbars are locked by manufacturers' shelf contracts and cannot carry 3,500 SKUs; over 70% of US and UK pouch users buy two or more brands a week; one-third of Haypp's sales are products that did not exist twelve months earlier. Each of these pushes heavy, variety-seeking users online.
  • Against Haypp (not yet). Manufacturer direct-to-consumer: about 1% of online volumes; single-brand sites cannot match a multi-brand range, and US state-by-state tax and flavour rules make direct selling costly. A senior PMI brand manager says manufacturers "should" own consumer relationships; I judge this a latent, not active, threat in the 18-month window. The precedent that matters is not a manufacturer website but supply withdrawal (PMI paused online ZYN in the US in 2024).
  • AI search (the real watch item). About 97% of Haypp's customers arrive organically. Large-language-model search traffic is growing 40 to 50% month on month in the US from a low base; such systems recommend two to five sites, which is winner-takes-most and so could reinforce the leader (Copilot recommends Northerner as the single third-party UK retailer) or, as Morgan Stanley's cross-sector work suggests, favour local providers. Either way it is a change to the cost of customer acquisition that Haypp does not control. The early warning is a falling organic share of new customers or rising paid marketing per new customer.
  • Quick-commerce (my hypothesis, not in the files). US delivery apps that deliver nicotine products in under an hour could attack the "immediacy" gap that keeps impulse users in stores; worth testing in the second set's research.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D4817th of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation is changing on many fronts at once, the near-term (18-month) net effect is mildly positive because most changes raise compliance barriers that Haypp clears and its rivals do not, but the distribution of outcomes has a fat left tail (EU cross-border online sales ban, US flavour bans, carrier shipping restrictions) that no forecast captures.

Reasoning, by jurisdiction:

  • US (largest growth market): positive federally, mixed locally. FDA enforcement discretion, MRTP orders and 39 new authorised SKUs since May 2026 widen Haypp's range. Twenty-one states plus DC tax pouches; New York's 75% wholesale tax adds about $2 a can at retail, and the CEO says such price shocks lift new-customer inflows online (online already 40 to 50% cheaper). Against this: state flavour bans (California exit after Q3 2024; Massachusetts not served), and municipal enforcement against Haypp's US subsidiary Northerner Scandinavia Inc (USD 2.5m San Francisco settlement, October 2025; a new USD 2.5m, about SEK 23.5m, provision for an unnamed city, Q3 2026). Two fines in a year say Haypp's US compliance is not yet best-in-class.
  • UK: tightening, net helpful to the leader. Tobacco and Vapes Act: purchase age 18 from late October 2026, free-sample and proxy-purchase bans, age notices at digital checkouts by 1 January 2027, and a full ban on nicotine advertising and sponsorship from 1 June 2027. Haypp will lose its paid levers (including its darts sponsorship) but rivals lose more, because Haypp's acquisition is organic. Plain packaging and a Nicotine Approval Scheme (from April 2028) are proposed.
  • EU: deferred tax, live product risk. The Tobacco Excise Directive was withdrawn (June 2026, Swedish veto) and the November 2026 attempt failed on Malta's veto; unanimity makes a high pouch tax unlikely before about 2030. But eight member states (October 2026) asked for flavour bans, nicotine caps, plain packaging and a ban on cross-border online sales; the Commission's TPD3 proposal is expected in early 2027, with application around 2029 to 2030. Austria already forced Haypp out (mid-2026, under 1% of sales).
  • Sweden and Norway (Core): friction. The Supreme Administrative Court ruled geo-located BankID insufficient for parcel-locker deliveries (October 2026); Haypp closed Snusnetto (about 500 customers) and deployed biometric verification at lockers. Norway bans flavour and strength descriptors on cans from 1 January 2027, which management expects to raise the value of online product information.

For the model: no regulatory revenue loss in the base case for 2027; a provision line for US municipal fines of roughly SEK 20 to 25m a year should be treated as recurring, not one-off.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

A782nd of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, materially. Three industry-level drivers are currently accelerating Haypp's demand together: pouch category growth with rapid product innovation, retail-price shocks from US state taxes, and the manufacturers' own push of innovation through the online channel.

Reasoning:

  • Category growth and innovation. The US pouch category is 59.9% of US oral tobacco (+8.1 percentage points in a year); past-30-day users doubled in two years to 8.3 million; BAT's Velo, Altria's on! Plus and PMI's ZYN Ultra are all new 2025 to 2026 formats; FDA authorised 39 new SKUs since May 2026, and Haypp added 68 US SKUs in Q3 2026. Innovation multiplies the variety advantage only an online range can satisfy.
  • Tax-driven price shocks. The CEO said (7 May 2026) that retail price increases drive a material uptick in new-customer inflows from the affected states; New York's 75% wholesale tax adds about $2 a can; most proposals are 30 to 35% of wholesale. The online discount widens in absolute terms when shelf prices rise.
  • Manufacturer channel economics. Online is margin-accretive for brand owners (physical US retail margins up to 50%), so they increasingly launch online first and pay for the visibility.

The counterweights: the ZYN 20-pouch can lowers revenue per pouch; manufacturer promotions lower Growth price/mix; state flavour bans remove states from Haypp's map. Net, the industry driver is accelerating demand; the Consumer should model Growth-segment volume growth well above US category growth (Haypp's UK volume growth of 93% was more than twice the market's).

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A761st of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand strongly supports the thesis: it is habitual, high-frequency, loyal to the platform once a second order is placed, and expanding in basket size and order frequency; it is fragile only to things that take away the online price or convenience advantage (delivery restrictions, cross-border bans, a supplier pulling a key brand), not to consumer sentiment.

Evidence:

  • Loyalty: returning customers above 90% of net sales; about 85% of customers who place a second order stay active; about 67% repurchase within 70 days; NPS 80 (2025) to 82 (Q4 2025, US).
  • Intensity: order frequency rises 42% and average order value 12% from first to fifth purchase; core Scandinavian customers use about 15 pouches a day; 40% of US and UK pouch users use eight or more a day.
  • Variety-seeking: 73% of US pouch users use two or more brands a week; heavy users rotate four to five products a week; the 2025 ZYN shortage showed consumers switch brands but, once ZYN returned, a "major portion" returned to it, which favours a multi-brand retailer.
  • Growth of base: active consumers +18.8% (682,000); Growth +54.7% (227,000); Core +6.4% (455,000). Total orders 1.452 million in Q3 2026; average order value SEK 739.
  • Fragility: immediacy (a Pacific Smoke expert: online captures only 15 to 20% of e-vapour because users want to buy when they run out; shipping fees deter); the US user threshold (3.6 pouches a day on average) means most US users are not yet online-ready; Swedish average purchase values fell in Q3 2026.
  • For the model: Core active consumers growing about 5% a year with rising spend per consumer; Growth active consumers growing about 30 to 40% in 2027.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D5015th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators are, on balance, helping Haypp in the near term by raising compliance barriers it clears and by taxing the physical channel; but Haypp's position depends on regulators continuing to permit remote, cross-border and parcel sales of nicotine, and that permission is contested in the EU and could be restricted locally anywhere. The position is moderately fragile: the probability of a severe adverse change in 18 months is low, the impact would be large, and the timing is unpredictable.

Summary by actor (detail in C-04):

  • Helping: US FDA (MRTP for ZYN; enforcement discretion; faster authorisations); US states taxing pouches (shelf-price shocks drive online migration); UK age and ad rules (hurt non-compliant small online rivals more); Swedish government (vetoed EU tax).
  • Hurting: US municipalities and states (fines; flavour bans in California and Massachusetts, which Haypp does not serve); Swedish courts (locker ruling); Austria (monopoly retail); Norway (label ban, which may help online); eight EU states (proposing cross-border online sales bans and flavour bans); UK (ad ban ends Haypp's paid acquisition levers; plain packaging proposals).
  • Fragility: the European Commission's TPD3 draft (early 2027) is the key dated risk; Deutsche Bank judges an EU-wide ban "highly unlikely" because 16 member states have legal frameworks. US federal posture could reverse with a future administration. For the model, carry an explicit scenario: EU cross-border ban affecting Germany and Switzerland (small) and a severe US flavour-ban scenario (large).
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

A801st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is many times Haypp's current revenue and is itself growing about 15 to 20% a year; it allows 20%-plus revenue growth for Haypp through 2028 and beyond without heroic share assumptions, provided US online penetration rises from about 3% toward high single digits.

Reasoning (my estimates from the files' figures):

  • Global pouch retail value about USD 11.2bn (2024), forecast about 21% value CAGR to 2029; the US is about 78% of value (about USD 8.7bn in 2024). Third-party forecasts of USD 6.95bn (2025) to USD 42.5bn (2033) imply similar growth.
  • If US online penetration rose from about 3% to about 10% of a US pouch market of perhaps USD 15bn by 2028 to 2030, the US online pool would be about USD 1.5bn; at Haypp's 80 to 85% share and online prices 40 to 50% below store prices (so revenue below the retail-value pool), US revenue could reach roughly SEK 6 to 10bn, against a Growth segment of about SEK 1.45bn in 2026. Even a move to 6 to 8% penetration supports Growth revenue growth of 30%-plus a year to 2028.
  • Management's target that the US delivers about 65% of incremental group revenue to 2028 and that Haypp's US total-market share rises from 2 to 3% to 4 to 5% is consistent with this.
  • Core (Sweden, Norway) is mature (36% and about 22% online penetration; 80 to 90% online share): mid-to-high single-digit growth from price, mix and Media and Insights.
  • Limits: daily consumption in the US is low (3.6 to 4 pouches); ZYN's 20-pouch cans lower revenue per pouch; UK online is about 7%.

Overall external picture

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C-6511th of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Haypp is past the trough of its revenue cycle and in a strong upswing (constant-currency growth of 24%, 27% and 26% in the first three quarters of 2026 after 7% organic in 2025), while its margin cycle is at or near its trough in H2 2026; the revenue upswing will moderate from Q4 2026 as the ZYN base effect ends, and the margin cycle should turn up from Q4 2026 if the announced cut in lower-funnel marketing holds.

Reasoning:

  • Revenue cycle. FY2024 +16%, FY2025 +4.6% reported (7% organic) during the ZYN absence, 2026 about +25%. Active consumers reached 682,000 in Q3 2026 (+18.8%); Growth active consumers 227,000 (+54.7%, decelerating sequentially per Deutsche Bank); Growth pouch volumes +83% in Q1 2026 and +63% in Q3 2026. The deceleration is visible already and will be sharper in Q4 2026 when the comparison includes ZYN's return quarter.
  • Margin cycle. Adjusted EBIT margin 3.7% (FY2024), 3.9% (FY2025), 2.3% for January to September 2026 and 2.2% in Q3 2026; adjusted EBITDA margin 4.5% in Q3 2026 against 5.6%. Marketing rose from 1.0% to 3.2% of sales year on year; US and UK organisations were built out (G&A flat sequentially in Q3, i.e., the build-out is complete). Management said it would reduce and relocate lower-funnel marketing in Q4 2026 because many paid-acquired customers would have arrived organically.
  • Turning signal. The Core margin has already turned (EBITDA +61% to SEK 104m). The Growth margin is the swing: Deutsche Bank models -4.5% for 2027 and +0.3% for 2028.

For the model: expect group revenue growth to slow to about 16 to 20% in 2027 (Core about 8 to 10%, Growth about 35 to 40%; consensus is 21%, with Growth at about +48%) and the adjusted EBIT margin to recover from about 2.2% (FY2026) toward 3 to 3.5% in 2027.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

A662nd of 24

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is clearly favourable at the value-chain level (customers are migrating into Haypp's channel and suppliers need that channel), favourable but more contested at the industry level (manufacturer rivalry helps volume and Media and Insights but lowers price per pouch), and only mildly favourable at the sector level, where regulation is a two-sided force with fat tails and the market regime is hostile to small-cap growth. Tailwinds are stronger than headwinds in the base case; the headwinds are low-probability and high-impact.

Taking the Sector Digest's four tobacco stories in turn (Assistant Portfolio Manager, 15 October 2026):

  1. Pouch scale decides who keeps the profit as price per pouch falls. For manufacturers this favours BAT and PMI; for Haypp, a falling price per pouch is a revenue headwind per can, but rivalry among the large makers funds Haypp's visibility income. Net: neutral to mildly positive for Haypp.
  2. Daily users buying online through Haypp (rated "Early" by the note). The strongest and most Haypp-specific tailwind; the note's own judgement is that Haypp "gains whichever brand wins", limited by the makers' power to cut supply.
  3. European tax deferred toward 2030 while product rules tighten. Tax relief is positive; product rules (flavours, cross-border online sales) are the main sector-level headwind and are dated (TPD3 proposal 2027).
  4. Buybacks as debt falls. Not relevant to Haypp, which pays no dividend and buys back no shares; it means Haypp's equity story competes for sector money against 6 to 10 times earnings manufacturers returning 8 to 13% of market value a year.

All together: value chain, strong tailwind (score in the high 70s); industry, moderate tailwind; sector and regulation, balanced with left-tail risk; macro, a valuation headwind. For the model the external forces justify revenue growth at the top of the 2028 target's annual range in 2026 (about 25%) and of roughly 16 to 20% a year in 2027 to 2028, which lands 2028 revenue at or slightly below the low end of the target (about SEK 6.6 to 7.0bn against the SEK 7.1bn floor), with a 10 to 15% probability scenario of a regulatory or supplier shock that cuts Growth revenue by a third for a year.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internally, Haypp is a stable management team executing an unchanged strategy with more intensity, after finishing a two-year infrastructure build. The defining internal change of 2026 is the marketing dial: turned up hard in H1 2026, partly admitted to be wasteful in Q3, and set to be turned down from Q4 2026. That dial, not any structural change, is the main determinant of 2027 EBIT. Positives (automation with spare capacity, Media and Insights growth, regional accountability, a clean balance sheet) are well founded and back-end loaded. Negatives are about quality rather than direction: recurring one-off costs, two US fines in a year, a paid director, cash bonuses outside adjusted profit to fund executive co-investment. There are no capital-return or M&A levers, and owners are not pushing for change. For the model: hold the strategy constant, make marketing intensity the explicit scenario variable, add a recurring SEK 20 to 40m a year of items affecting comparability below adjusted EBIT, and do not model shareholder returns before 2029.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement36C-1455C-8638C-5958C-8134D-0336
Group judgement

Risks

F3620th of 24

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Haypp combines a low risk of gradual business deterioration (demand is habitual, the growth engine is accelerating, competitors are weak) with a high risk of sudden events and a high valuation exposure. The fundamental risks are concentrated at two chokepoints: supplier permission (three manufacturers, one of which has already pulled its leading brand) and regulatory permission for remote delivery of nicotine (US states and carriers, Swedish courts, the EU). They are correlated and, in my judgement, carry a combined 15 to 25% chance of at least one material event in 18 months. On top sits sentiment risk: an illiquid, thinly covered, partly exclusion-listed stock at the 83rd to 87th percentile of its own multiple, in a market regime selling small-cap growth. A fair trough is about 40 to 48% below today on multiple compression alone and 55 to 60% below with an EPS cut. For the second set, the risk picture argues for explicit scenario branches (supplier dispute; US flavour or delivery restriction) rather than a single-path forecast, and for weighting the predictability judgement down even where the base-case operating model looks sound.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

F5521st of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: operational and technical standards have improved clearly and measurably (automation, systems, fulfilment cost per unit); commercial cost discipline weakened in 2026 and is now being corrected; compliance and governance standards show lapses that have not yet improved.

Reasoning:

  • Improving. ERP (Project Jarvis) and middleware rolled out to all markets with no consumer impact (US go-live 1 March 2025); storefront migration to the V3 platform completed 8 December 2025. Automated warehouses in Stockholm, Oslo, Texas (Houston; one-third utilised, able to take three to four times current US volume without new capex) and Milton Keynes (June 2026; capacity quadrupled, four core staff, accuracy above 99%). Fulfilment was 1.7% of sales in Q3 2026; cost per can fell 6% while volumes grew 22% and fulfilment cost 14%. Warehouse cost fell from about 5% to 2% of order value as lead times fell from about 20 to 4 hours. G&A fell to 10.4% of sales in Q3 from 11.3% in Q2 and was flat in absolute terms. Inventory normalised to SEK 315m, net working capital to SEK 169m, operating cash flow SEK 212.8m for nine months (SEK 152.2m).
  • Weakened, then corrected. Paid lower-funnel marketing was partly wasted (management's own admission). Adjusted EBIT margin fell 270 basis points in Q1 2026.
  • Lapses. Two US municipal fines (USD 2.5m each); Swedish licence dispute lost at the Supreme Administrative Court; items affecting comparability every year (SEK 30.4m in 2024, SEK 52.6m in 2025, SEK 52.7m in Q3 2026 alone, including discretionary bonuses to fund executives' participation in the incentive plan); a paid consultant on the board; an audit committee that is the whole board, chaired by the board chair. Employee turnover improved sharply (52.7% in 2023 to 10.5% in 2025) but satisfaction fell from 87% (2021) to 80% (2023 to 2024).

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C589th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the culture is congruent in its core (low-cost, customer-value, data-led, fast-moving, owner-aligned) and the system fits together: scale economies shared, organic acquisition, automated fulfilment and data monetisation reinforce one another. Congruence weakens at the edges: compliance culture lags commercial ambition, and governance and pay practices (paid director, bonuses outside adjusted profit) are not fully aligned with the low-cost, shareholder-aligned ethos.

Evidence:

  • Congruent: flat, open, autonomous culture praised by former employees; executives sit with staff; rigorous hiring; owner-level shareholdings by the CEO and founders; reinvestment of all cash; price leadership.
  • Strains: employee satisfaction fell from 87% (2021) to 80% (2023 to 2024), and code-of-conduct compliance from about 100% to 80% (2024); historic turnover of 52.7% in 2023; a lean central team managing many regulatory regimes; the CEO's reluctance to delegate.
  • Comparison with the Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair standard: Haypp has Ryanair's low-cost-share-the-savings logic but not yet its relentless cost and compliance discipline or its precision in communication.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F3421st of 24

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Haypp carries an unusually dense cluster of sudden, high-impact risks for a company with staple-like demand, because its whole business sits at two chokepoints (supplier permission and regulatory permission for remote delivery). On my judgement the combined probability that at least one of the major ones hits within 18 months is roughly 15 to 25%; the largest single exposures are a supplier withdrawal and a US delivery or flavour restriction. These are correlated with each other (regulatory pressure can prompt suppliers to restrict online sales, as in 2024).

Ranked by expected damage (probabilities are my judgements over 18 months; magnitudes against the 2027 base):

  1. A major manufacturer withdraws a key brand or materially worsens online terms (10 to 15%). Precedent: PMI's 2024 to 2025 ZYN pause (46% of US sales; Growth revenue -26% in Q2 2025). Impact: Growth revenue down 20 to 30% for a year; Media and Insights income down; 2027 EBIT could halve.
  2. US state flavour bans in large states or a federal flavour restriction (5 to 10%). Haypp already exits banned states (California, Massachusetts); a ban in Texas, Florida or New York would remove a large share of the US addressable market (the Q2 2025 Earnings Review judged bans in California, Texas or Florida could eliminate over 40% of the US digital market).
  3. US carrier or postal restriction on shipping nicotine pouches, or new delivery-verification mandates (3 to 5%). Called existential by a Reynolds American expert; the Swedish locker ruling shows how quickly delivery rules can change.
  4. Core (Sweden and Norway) regulatory tightening, for example on remote age verification, home delivery or licences (5 to 10%). Core produces all group profit; Swedish licence revocations in 2024 and 2026 show the authorities' willingness to act.
  5. EU cross-border online sales ban via TPD3 (under 5% within 18 months; higher by 2029 to 2030). Direct exposure is limited (Germany and Switzerland; Austria already exited), but it would set a precedent.
  6. A large US fine or youth-access litigation (10 to 15%; magnitude SEK 25 to 100m). Two USD 2.5m provisions in twelve months.
  7. A health scare or study damaging the pouch category (low; magnitude category-wide).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–36

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly concentrated, more so than in the past, and the few drivers are linked to each other. The difference between the next four years and the last six is made by three things: supplier-paid income, restraint in costs, and the number of Growth customers. They share one root, the manufacturers' rivalry for the online customer and their permission to reach that customer through Haypp. The offsetting fact is that the concentrated drivers are not binary in the base case: each has a management lever that trades profit for growth, and the mature segment alone earns more than the group.

How concentrated, in numbers (2026 to 2030, from the second set's own driver table):

  • By income stream. Gross profit rises by about SEK 730m. Media and Insights supplies about SEK 690m of that (SEK 570m to about SEK 1,260m); gross profit on products supplies about SEK 45m. One stream that is 12% of revenue provides about 94% of the growth in gross profit and one and a half times the growth in EBITDA (SEK 461m). In 2023 to 2026, by contrast, gross profit on products rose substantially as well (the product margin rose to 8.4% from, on my rough estimate, 4 to 5%, on better buying terms); the forecast has no further help from product margin. As D-01 explains, the 94% is partly a choice (giving product margin away) and the gross-margin input needs less; but on any reading the growth in profit is supplier-paid.
  • By payer. Three manufacturers hold about 87% of pouch volume. Contracts are annual, for the calendar year, with quarterly projects on top.
  • By segment. Two-thirds of the EBITDA increase (SEK 306m of SEK 461m) is the Growth segment moving from a loss of SEK 144m to a profit of SEK 162m; within Growth, the United States dominates.
  • By line. On the cost side the change is one line, general and administrative costs, falling from 10.7% to 8.4% of sales (2.3 of the 3.8 points of margin gain).
  • By time. About 80% of the increase in adjusted EBIT arrives in 2028 to 2030.

Why the drivers are one cluster rather than three: supplier income follows the customer base with a lag of up to a year (annual contracts); the customer base follows range, price and availability, which the same suppliers control; cost restraint is easier when supplier income is strong and tempting to abandon when the window is open. A supplier dispute would hit all three at once: in Q2 2025 Growth revenue fell 26% and Growth active consumers 34% when ZYN was absent.

What limits the damage that concentration can do:

  • Levers that trade profit for growth. Marketing (about SEK 120m a year, cuttable to about SEK 55m), price reinvestment (the product-margin cushion, about SEK 50m in 2027), Saudi Arabia (about SEK 16m). Together these are about SEK 130m, two-thirds of 2027 adjusted EBIT. They do not help in a supplier withdrawal.
  • Core. About SEK 364m of EBITDA in 2026 against SEK 220m for the group; 455,000 habitual customers; two countries where Haypp holds 80 to 90% of online sales.
  • Supplier rivalry is rising into 2027, not falling. The research report on manufacturers' budgets found Philip Morris raising United States selling costs by about 10% in Q4 2026 behind ZYN, BAT building capacity for a billion cans and taking Velo to about 30% of volume, Altria spending enough behind on! PLUS that Deutsche Bank expects a further point off its oral margin in 2027, and new entrants (Imperial with Helwit, Japan Tobacco with CLEW, Turning Point, Scandinavian Tobacco Group launching online first). The FDA authorised 24 Velo products in autumn 2026. Each launch is a buyer of visibility. The concentration is therefore a medium-term exposure more than an 18-month one; the dated 18-month test is the 2027 contract round, visible in the reports of February and May 2027.

Against the past: the last six years were driven by many things at once (acquisitions, the technology rebuild, new countries, supply shocks, licence disputes). The next four are simpler, which helps tracking, and narrower, which removes diversification. Simplicity is worth something to an investor who can watch three numbers; it is worth less when all three answer to the same counterparties.

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5523rd of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy (replicate the Nordic online model in the US and UK) has not changed; the priority and intensity have, twice in eighteen months: from "prudent spending until regulation is clear" (Q2 2025) to an aggressive paid-marketing land grab (2026), and then, at Q3 2026, to a partial pull-back from lower-funnel paid marketing toward price and product. The change is well resourced (cash from Core, an undrawn SEK 400m facility) but only loosely articulated, because the marketing budget and its expected returns are not disclosed.

Reasoning:

  • 2026: after FDA enforcement discretion and the return of ZYN, marketing rose to 1.8% of sales in Q2 and 3.2% (SEK 39m) in Q3, plus the PDC darts sponsorship in the UK, while the "window remains open" before the UK advertising ban (June 2027).
  • Q3 2026: management said analysis showed many customers acquired through paid lower-funnel channels would have found Haypp organically, and that Q4 spend would be reduced and redirected to the consumer offer and launches; it declined to quantify the change.
  • Other priority changes: segment simplification to Core and Growth (January 2026); exits from UK vape (late 2025, SEK 6 to 7m annual savings), Austria (mid-2026) and German vape/heat-not-burn; Saudi Arabia entry (July 2026, about SEK 4m in Q3) with PIF-owned DZRT.
  • What this tells the Consumer. The Q3 admission is valuable: it shows management measures incrementality and corrects. It also shows that the 2026 margin trough was partly self-inflicted overspending, and that the marketing dial is the single largest driver of 2027 EBIT. Because the budget is undisclosed, the second set must model marketing as an explicit, scenario-driven line (base: fall from 3.2% of sales in Q3 2026 to about 2.5% for 2027 and about 2% for 2028).
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D4517th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: targets have been set, not raised. The April 2025 capital markets day replaced a missed target (SEK 5bn sales in 2025; actual SEK 3.85bn, 23% short) with new 2028 targets: constant-currency revenue CAGR of 18 to 25% from 2024 (roughly doubling 2024 sales of SEK 3.68bn) and an adjusted EBIT margin of 5.5% plus or minus 1.5 percentage points, with a stated ambition of three- to four-fold adjusted EBIT from 2024. They were reiterated at Q3 2026. There is no annual guidance.

Reasoning:

  • Arithmetic of the targets. At 18% CAGR, 2028 revenue is about SEK 7.1bn; at 25% about SEK 9.0bn. Three- to four-fold the 2024 adjusted EBIT of SEK 134.5m is about SEK 400 to 540m. At 5.5% of SEK 7.1 to 9.0bn, EBIT is SEK 390 to 495m.
  • Where consensus sits. FY2028 consensus revenue SEK 6.88bn is about 4% below the 18% floor (before currency effects); FY2028 consensus EBIT SEK 363m (5.3% margin) is inside the margin range but below the three- to four-fold EBIT ambition. So the company's targets are above consensus on both revenue and absolute EBIT.
  • Character of the targets. Because 2025 grew only about 7% organically, the four-year target is harder than the 2026 run-rate suggests: from about SEK 4.8bn in 2026, reaching the 18% floor (SEK 7.1bn) needs about 22% growth in each of 2027 and 2028, the midpoint (about SEK 8.0bn) about 29%, and the top (SEK 9.0bn) about 37%. With the ZYN base effect ending in Q4 2026, only the low end looks achievable on current evidence. The margin target is wide (4.0 to 7.0%) and leaves room for the discretionary marketing choice. The previous target set (2025) was missed, and the target date was moved rather than the target hit.

For the model: treat the 2028 targets as management's stated ceiling-ish base case; consensus is slightly more cautious; no raise is likely before a new CMD.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

C558th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the company is steadily pruning small, low-return or regulatorily hostile units, which simplifies the business and improves predictability at the margin; the amounts are small (each under 2% of sales), so the financial effect is modest but positive.

Reasoning:

  • UK vape and heat-not-burn discontinued (late 2025) because illicit products made the category unprofitable: about a 2 percentage point drag on Q3 2026 constant-currency growth, SEK 6 to 7m annual cost savings.
  • Austria exited mid-2026 (pouches restricted to the licensed tobacconist monopoly; under 1% of group sales).
  • German vape and heat-not-burn being discontinued; Swedish vape moved into Core.
  • Snusnetto (about 500 customers, under 0.05% of sales) closed after the Swedish court ruling.
  • The "Emerging" segment was abolished in January 2026.
  • Effect: Haypp is becoming a purer nicotine-pouch retailer (pouches 72% of volumes in Q3 2026) with fewer categories exposed to illicit trade. For the model, remove about 2 percentage points of UK vape revenue from the 2026 base and do not expect material further disposals.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F5519th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: geographic entry is sensible and cheap: the big bets (US, UK) are existing markets being scaled, and the one new market (Saudi Arabia) is a small, staged option in the world's second-largest pouch market by volume. Product extension is limited and sensible (moist pouch launches, subscriptions, sensory data panels). The risk is not recklessness but distraction while the US is unprofitable.

Reasoning:

  • Saudi Arabia. Launched July 2026 with the PIF-owned DZRT brand; management sizes the market at about 170 million cans in 2026; about SEK 4m invested in Q3 2026 (about 35 basis points of group margin in the quarter); category opening to other brands is uncertain until at least late 2027. The CEO says sales are "growing exponentially" from a low base and calls it a long-term investment. A single-brand monopoly limits Haypp's core advantage (range), so the value depends on liberalisation.
  • US and UK scaling. The US is expected to contribute about 65% of incremental group revenue to 2028; UK expected to be Europe's largest pouch market by 2030; online share in the UK is about 7%.
  • Next candidates. A former head of accounting names Spain as a target; Haypp MEA Holdings was registered in December 2025. Europe beyond the five current countries is limited by hostile national rules.
  • Products. Online-first launches (ZYN Ultra), 68 new US SKUs in Q3 2026, Scandinavian subscriptions growing "exponentially", new sensory panels for Media and Insights in 2027.

For the model: Saudi Arabia should be modelled as a small cost (SEK 10 to 20m a year) with option value, not as revenue.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F4219th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Haypp does not raise prices in real terms; it is a price-taker whose shelf prices follow manufacturers' list prices and taxes, and whose strategy is to share scale economies through a 20 to 50% discount to physical retail. Price rises in Core are nominal pass-through; in Growth, price/mix is negative. Real pricing power exists only in latent form: the option to narrow the discount, which management has not signalled.

Reasoning:

  • Core: price/mix "meaningfully positive" as manufacturer price increases and the January 2026 Swedish excise were passed through; average order value rose to SEK 739 from SEK 689 group-wide. That lifts revenue and absolute gross profit at a stable percentage margin; it is not Haypp's own pricing.
  • Growth: price/mix negative in aggregate on manufacturer-funded promotions (Q2 2026 Growth gross margin 18.9% against 24.4%).
  • Latent lever: Deutsche Bank (April 2026) argues Haypp "can quickly unwind consumer discounts to lock in profits" if momentum slows. The discount gap to physical retail is large (40 to 50% in the US), so a 2 to 3 percentage point narrowing would not destroy the value proposition and would add materially to margin (each 1 percentage point of US price on SEK 2bn of Growth revenue is about SEK 20m). Management has not mentioned this; an expert from Reynolds American says brand owners' contracts limit online retailers' pricing power.
  • Where Haypp's "price" actually lives: in Media and Insights fees charged to manufacturers, growing about 38 to 41%. That is the de facto pricing lever.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5015th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A is not stepping up; it is small, tactical and sensible (licence protection and local consolidation in Sweden). It is not a material driver for the model.

Reasoning:

  • Snusvaruhuset Sweden AB acquired February 2025 (purchase price recorded as goodwill; SEK -0.6m contribution in the period); a small Swedish cigar company bought to secure a replacement tobacco licence during the 2024 licence dispute, which protected about 80% of Swedish revenue.
  • Past: Northerner (US) acquired September 2019, the platform for the US business; 2021 to 2023 integration costs drove negative free cash flow in 2022 and 2023.
  • Stated policy (December 2025): open to selective acquisitions to consolidate existing markets or enter new ones. Acquisition outflows were SEK 1.5m (2024), SEK 2.4m (2025) and zero in 2026 to date.
  • Given that rivals are tiny and failing, buying them adds little; the logical larger deal would be a US online competitor (Prilla, about one-tenth of Haypp's US scale) for customers and SEO assets. Not in base case.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5016th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: no shareholder returns are planned (all free cash flow is reinvested; no dividend; no buyback), and the balance sheet is used conservatively: the step-up in "investment" is going through the income statement as marketing and personnel, funded by Core cash. Management is not mooting a change. This is appropriate for the opportunity but removes a lever peers use to lift EPS.

Reasoning:

  • Net debt SEK 96.3m at Q3 2026, all leases; net debt to LTM adjusted EBITDA 0.4x; a new SEK 400m multicurrency revolving credit facility with Danske Bank (August 2026) replaced a SEK 170m overdraft; lease liabilities were SEK 88.8m non-current and SEK 33.4m current at September 2026, up on the new UK warehouse and US office.
  • The board "does not expect to distribute dividends" and reinvests all free cash flow; consensus dividends are zero through FY2029.
  • Share count: 31.8m; a March 2026 warrant tender issued 502,402 new shares at SEK 125 in exchange for 775,000 warrants (dilution reduced to 1.6% after the deal); LTIP 2026/2029 dilution about 3% if hurdles (up to SEK 177.66) are met.
  • For the model: share count drifts up about 1 to 2% over three years; net finance cost stays at about SEK 10 to 15m plus currency revaluation noise; no buybacks. A first capital return is plausible only after the Growth segment turns profitable (2028 at the earliest); I would not include it.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–62

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: three positive corporate changes are under way that the market may under-weight: (1) Media and Insights is becoming a deeper, more productised data business (sensory panels from 2027, launch testing embedded in manufacturers' plans); (2) the completed infrastructure gives very high incremental margins on volume (Texas at one-third capacity); (3) the reallocation from paid acquisition to price and product could lift margin without hurting growth, if management's incrementality finding is right.

Reasoning:

  • Media and Insights. About 12.2% of revenue in Q3 2026 (11.1% a year earlier), growing about 38 to 41%, more than half of gross profit; data said to lead offline trends by 12 to 18 months; Imperial Brands uses Haypp as a volume-share benchmark alongside Nielsen. A larger customer base makes the inventory more valuable to brand owners, a self-reinforcing loop.
  • Operating leverage on fixed infrastructure. Capex should fall as the build-out ends (Deutsche Bank); Texas and Milton Keynes can absorb multiples of current volume.
  • Subscription and retention. Subscriptions growing fast in Scandinavia; the US loyalty programme restructuring drove 60% growth in active members (Q2 2025); NPS about 80 to 82; about 85% of second-time buyers stay active.
  • AI tools. HayppWriter automates SEO content across 16 storefronts; AI in central functions is expected to hold G&A growth below revenue growth.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5012th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: none have. The register is concentrated in founders, venture funds and a few institutions that support the growth strategy, and there is no activist. Change in approach would have to come from management or from an outside bidder.

Reasoning:

  • Holders: GR8 Ventures 12.62%, Patrik Rees (co-founder, director) 11.68%, Fidelity 9.87%, Northerner Holding 9.63% (the vendors of the US business), Robotti 5.41%; the CEO owns about 896,500 shares (2.75%). The nomination committee represents the largest owners.
  • No activist campaigns or public demands (AlphaSense search).
  • DNB Carnegie noted in 2024 that the company could at some point attract private equity or industrial bids (dominant online share, low debt). An industrial buyer (a manufacturer) would be a competition-law and channel-neutrality problem; private equity is the more plausible. I do not put a bid in the base case, but it is a real support under the share price at depressed multiples.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5620th of 24

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are real and well resourced but narrow: they are almost entirely about executing one strategy harder (US and UK scaling, automation, Media and Insights) rather than about new capital-allocation levers or a new management standard. Their financial effect is back-end loaded (2027 to 2028) and depends on a single discretionary choice (how fast marketing intensity falls).

Reasoning:

  • Strong: infrastructure completed and paid for; Core margin at a record; a regional management layer in place; Media and Insights growing 40%; balance sheet un-levered with a new facility.
  • Narrow: no capital returns, no M&A, no pricing change; guidance limited to a wide 2028 range.
  • Offsets: recurring one-off costs, compliance lapses and governance softness (C-86).
  • Synthesis for the model: the corporate changes support the margin recovery that consensus already models (group EBITDA from about 4.6% in 2026 to 5.7% in 2027 and 7.2% in 2028); they do not, on their own, justify forecasting above consensus. The upside case requires management to cut marketing faster than consensus assumes without losing customer growth.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C606th of 24

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: there is a reasonable but not rich staircase. The single most novel and valuable step is the first evidence that the Growth segment's losses are narrowing while it still grows fast (most likely visible between Q4 2026 and Q3 2027); everything else is incremental or regulatory, and the regulatory steps cut both ways. The near-term step (Q4 2026 results, 10 February 2027) is well-founded in management's stated actions; the later steps depend on execution and on regulators.

Next 6 months (to about May 2027):

  • Q4 2026 results, 10 February 2027: first quarter of reduced lower-funnel marketing; test of margin recovery against the first full-ZYN comparison. Novelty: medium (a turn in the margin trend); foundation: management's explicit Q4 plan. Risk: the growth rate will fall visibly, which the market may read negatively.
  • US municipal settlement: removes a provision overhang; small, positive, unfounded on timing.
  • Norway label ban (1 January 2027) and UK digital age notices (1 January 2027): management expects online information to gain value; low novelty.
  • New manufacturer launches online first (Velo Max, ZYN Ultra extensions, FDA authorisations): continuing, low novelty.
  • Negative step in the window: the European Commission's TPD3 proposal (early 2027).
  • Q1 2027 results (about early May 2027).

Months 6 to 18 (about May 2027 to May 2028):

  • UK advertising and sponsorship ban from 1 June 2027: Haypp cuts UK marketing; weaker rivals lose their channel; consolidation toward the organic leader. Novelty: medium; foundation: law passed.
  • Growth-segment losses narrowing through H2 2027 (Deutsche Bank: elevated investment "through mid-2027"): the most novel step; Growth has never been run at scale profitably. Foundation: medium.
  • Media and Insights sensory panels (2027) and continued 30 to 40% Media and Insights growth: gross-margin support; medium novelty.
  • Saudi Arabia category opening (late 2027 at the earliest): option; low foundation.
  • A possible capital markets day with 2030 targets (my conjecture, based on the 2023 and 2025 pattern): could reframe the story beyond 2028.

Months 18 to 36 (about mid-2028 to late 2029):

  • Growth segment at or above breakeven and the 2028 targets tested (results in early 2029). High novelty if achieved; medium foundation.
  • First possible capital-return discussion once Growth is self-funding; low foundation.
  • Headwinds in the same window: EU TPD3 application (2029 to 2030), UK Nicotine Approval Scheme (from April 2028), a translation headwind if USD/SEK falls toward Nordea's 9.29 by end-2028.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B635th of 24

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B635th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Haypp sits in a near-monopoly position in a small but fast-growing channel (online), surrounded by a hostile but mutually dependent supply base (three manufacturers holding about 87% of pouch volume) and a regulatory perimeter that mostly raises its barriers but could, in a few tail cases, close parts of its channel. Every force except supplier power is favourable: rivals are weak and failing, consolidation runs toward the leader, customers are habitual, substitutes (stores) are structurally losing heavy users, regulation tightens against non-compliant rivals. The supplier relationship is the crux: manufacturers both fund Haypp's profit (Media and Insights, promotions, online-first launches) and can withdraw its volume (the 2024 ZYN pause). Rivalry among manufacturers is currently good for Haypp; a calmer or more consolidated manufacturer structure would be worse. For the model: hold online share, model total-market share gains from 2 to 3% toward 4 to 5% in the US by 2028, keep Growth price/mix negative until mid-2027, and carry two explicit tail scenarios (supplier dispute; regulatory channel restriction).

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?A761st of 24full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D5616th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Haypp is itself the route to market for the manufacturers; the intermediaries that matter to Haypp are Google (search), the parcel carriers and locker networks (delivery) and the age-verification and payment providers (compliance). All three currently help, but two of them (search and delivery) are single points of failure that Haypp does not control. Fragility is moderate and rising for search, low-probability but severe for delivery.

Reasoning:

  • Search: about 97% of customers arrive organically; HayppWriter automates content across 16 storefronts; brand building aims to secure visibility in large-language-model answers. A search algorithm change or the rise of AI "zero-click" answers could raise acquisition costs. The Q3 2026 finding that paid lower-funnel customers would have come organically confirms organic search is still the dominant channel.
  • Delivery: commoditised through the Ingrid shipping platform and multiple carriers; same-day delivery piloted in Houston; free UK shipping above £15. Risk: regulators or carriers restricting nicotine shipments (the Reynolds American expert's "existential" risk), or court-imposed delivery verification (the Swedish locker ruling). Haypp's adaptation in Sweden (biometric lockers within weeks) shows resilience.
  • Payments and verification: integrated (Klarna, SAP; AWS data platform). Generalist marketplaces (Amazon) stay out because multistep age checks and adult signatures break their one-click model; that protects Haypp.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

A741st of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving in a way that helps Haypp. Online rivals are small, paid-acquisition dependent and about to lose their main channel in the UK; the physical stores are being squeezed by rising taxes and limited shelf space; manufacturer direct sites are marginal. Haypp's position against existing competitors is robust; its fragility lies elsewhere (suppliers, regulators).

Evidence:

  • Online rivals: Prilla (US, about one-tenth of Haypp), Snus Vikings and Two Wombats (UK) rely on paid advertising; UK ad ban (June 2027) and compliance rules raise their costs; Haypp's SEO and scale cost advantage widens.
  • Physical retail: US convenience stores earn up to 50% margins on pouches, leaving the 40 to 50% online discount sustainable; state taxes raise shelf prices; UK independent vape shops lose business-rate relief in April 2027; a Pacific Smoke expert notes immediacy keeps impulse buyers in stores.
  • Manufacturer direct-to-consumer: about 1% of online volume; single-brand portfolios cannot satisfy multi-brand users.
  • Promotion behaviour: manufacturers' promotions run through Haypp as well as through stores, so they do not shift share away from Haypp.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D5015th of 24full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

B+603rd of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the disruption forces act most heavily on the physical retailer (losing heavy users and facing taxes), on the combustible manufacturers (losing smokers to pouches) and on Haypp's small online rivals (regulation and ad bans); they act on Haypp itself mainly through two channels it does not control: search discovery and supplier strategy. Net, the disruption forces leave Haypp's position stronger but with two concentrated dependencies.

By actor:

  • Demand (consumers): migration from cigarettes to pouches, from stores to online, from single-brand loyalty to multi-brand rotation: strengthens Haypp.
  • Competition (online rivals): squeezed by compliance costs and ad bans: strengthens Haypp.
  • Physical retail: taxes, shelf limits, store closures (UK vape shops lose rate relief 2027): strengthens Haypp.
  • Manufacturers: shifting margin mix toward pouches; rivalry for share; some interest in direct relationships; capacity added; this both funds Haypp (Media and Insights, promotions) and threatens it (supply power, potential disintermediation).
  • Regulators: tightening product and delivery rules: strengthens Haypp against rivals but creates tail risk to the whole channel.
  • Search platforms and AI: changing discovery; the most important unpriced change to Haypp's own cost structure.
