C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

A821st of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Haypp is the clear number one online nicotine-pouch retailer in every market it serves, by a very wide margin; it is a sub-scale player in total pouch retail, where convenience stores dominate. Its size advantage over the number two online retailer is so large (about 10 to 14 times) that rank is not a question; the question is how fast the online channel grows within the total market.

Evidence:

  • Online share: about 80 to 90% in Sweden and Norway; about 85% of US online pouch sales (Deutsche Bank; DNB Carnegie said 55% in November 2025, so the US figure is uncertain); about 65% of UK online pouch sales.
  • Relative size: about 14 times the nearest global online competitor; about 10 times Prilla in the US.
  • Total-market share: US 2 to 3% (2024) on management's figures; physical retail still holds over 90% of global nicotine volume and over 50% of modern oral purchases in key growth markets; e-commerce is about 5% of the US modern oral market (Deutsche Bank, August 2025).
  • Customers: 682,000 active consumers (Q3 2026); the 2025 annual figure of 1.131 million is a different (twelve-month) definition and should not be compared with the quarterly figure.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

B+703rd of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: both of Haypp's markets are highly concentrated, but in opposite ways that matter: online retail is concentrated in Haypp's favour (one player holds about 80 to 90%, the next a small fraction); manufacturing is concentrated against Haypp (three groups hold about 87 to 88% of pouch volumes). The top five online retailers certainly hold more than 30%, and the distribution is extremely steep (Haypp alone holds several times the share of numbers two to ten combined).

Evidence:

  • Online: Haypp 80 to 90% in its core territories; rivals are small, single-country and often loss-making.
  • Manufacturing: PMI, BAT and Altria held 87.6% of US pouch volumes in 2025 (88.4% in 2020); ZYN holds about 57% of US modern oral retail value; Velo 54% of volume outside the US; the top three consolidated 90% of US pouch volumes by 2024 (Jefferies).
  • Physical retail: fragmented (convenience chains), but manufacturers control backbar space through share-based shelf contracts.
  • Implication for the model: Haypp has pricing-to-consumer freedom against retail rivals but not against suppliers; its gross margin depends on negotiated terms with three counterparties that are each larger by two orders of magnitude.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

A741st of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Haypp's online share has been stable to rising for five or more years and new online entrants have taken negligible share (roughly 60 have failed); manufacturer brand shares, by contrast, are unstable (Velo took about 13 percentage points of US volume share in a year), which is good for Haypp because brand churn drives customers to a multi-brand retailer.

Evidence:

  • Online: 90% Sweden and 80% Norway in 2021 (RedEye) against 80 to 90% now; the US online lead persisted through the 2024 to 2025 ZYN absence; UK new entrants (Snus Vikings, Two Wombats) depend on paid social media and will be hit by the June 2027 advertising ban.
  • New entrant precedent: online rivals "fall into a financial dark hole" without Haypp's scale (CEO); manufacturer direct sites hold about 1% of online volume; zyn.com, run by Scandinavian Tobacco Group, was suspended in June 2024 after a regulatory subpoena.
  • Manufacturer share churn: Velo US volume share 16.2% (2025) to 28 to 30% (H1 2026); Swedish shelves carry over 50 brands; around 450 pouch brands compete offline in the UK; about 200 new competitors launch monthly (BAT digest).
  • For the model: assume Haypp's online share is held through 2028; the risk to share is not a retail entrant but a channel shift (manufacturer supply decisions, quick-commerce, AI agents).
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

A781st of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: yes, strongly, and Haypp is the beneficiary. Online nicotine retail consolidates toward the operator with the lowest customer acquisition cost (organic search authority), the widest range, the best procurement terms and the most compliance infrastructure; Haypp leads on all four, and each regulatory tightening raises the bar further.

Mechanisms:

  • Customer acquisition: 97% organic acquisition, historically keeping marketing below 0.5% of sales; 40% of new customers come from word of mouth.
  • Range and procurement: about 3,500 SKUs, eight times a typical store; purchasing scale secures better terms than smaller websites; at least a 10% price advantage over manufacturer direct sites.
  • Compliance: biometric locker verification built in six months; age verification at checkout and delivery; voluntary 20mg cap; collaboration with the British Standards Institution. Regulatory tightening (UK age rules and ad ban, Swedish locker ruling, US state directories) hardens the moat of the compliant incumbent; this is the Wisdom Library's "regulatory moat hardening" pattern (licensing tightening that consolidates share toward surviving incumbents).
  • Data: a larger base makes Media and Insights more valuable to manufacturers, which funds price investment.
  • The counter-force: the most "advantaged" actor in the whole chain is not the largest retailer but the brand owner, who can withhold supply. Within retail, consolidation toward Haypp is near-certain; across the chain, power stays upstream.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5513th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: among manufacturers, the historical attitude (margin first, disciplined pricing) is currently suspended in pouches: the large makers are in a volume-first land grab in the US, funding promotions and accepting low category margins (BAT's new categories contribution margin 13.8% against 43.7% in combustibles). Among online retailers, small rivals are volume-first but too weak to matter. For Haypp this creates a mixed but manageable environment: falling price per pouch and negative Growth price/mix, offset by manufacturer-funded promotions and Media and Insights.

Evidence:

  • Manufacturers: Velo gains through promotional discounting; Altria's on! cut promotions and raised price 21.6% (Q3 2026) after losing share; ZYN cut its price per pouch by moving to 20-pouch cans; competitor retail prices fell 12% year on year (late 2025). Jefferies argues a two-to-three-player structure will restore pricing discipline; a Circle K category manager warns promotions may be permanent.
  • Retailers: UK and US challengers offer single-can discounts; Haypp's response is not to match tactically but to rely on structural price advantage.
  • Falling volumes and falling prices: not applicable for pouches (volumes rising fast). In combustibles, prices rise as volumes fall, the opposite pattern.
  • For the model: assume Growth price/mix stays negative through mid-2027 and turns flat to positive in 2028 as the makers seek returns (Jefferies models US modern-oral contribution margins rising from 5% in 2025 to 57% by 2030 for BAT, which implies makers will eventually withdraw promotional support).
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F4218th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the manufacturers' attitude has been unstable over three years (from scarcity and allocation in 2024 to 2025 to promotion-led share battles in 2026), and one of them has already done the equivalent of a shark attack on Haypp, not on pricing but on supply: PMI's 2024 decision to pause online ZYN sales in the US. Retail rivals cannot attack Haypp on price; the manufacturers could attack it through supply, terms or a direct channel if they judged Haypp's power over their consumers too great.

Evidence and reasoning:

  • 2024 to 2025: ZYN shortage and PMI's online pause (after a District of Columbia subpoena) removed 46% of Haypp's US sales; Growth revenue fell 26% (Q2 2025); consumers sampled on!, FRE and ALP; a direct PMI procurement contract was signed in September 2025 "utilising Media and Insights services".
  • 2026: oversupply (new PMI capacity), promotion battles, rapid product launches.
  • Would they attack? Their incentive is balanced: online saves them about 15% of margin against physical retail and is their test-launch channel, but a dominant retailer owning the consumer data and 85% of a fast-growing channel is exactly what brand owners resist over time. The Reynolds American expert says brand owners limit online retailers' pricing power through contracts.
  • For the model: no base-case attack; a 10 to 15% probability per year that one major maker restricts supply or terms in a market, with a modelled impact similar to 2024 to 2025 (a temporary loss of a third or more of that market's revenue growth).
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C+628th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is supportive for Haypp at the retail level (near-monopoly online, high and rising barriers, consolidation toward the leader) and unsupportive at the supply level (three dominant suppliers who also pay much of Haypp's gross profit). Support is moderately fragile: it rests on a mutually beneficial but asymmetric relationship with the manufacturers, and on regulators continuing to allow cross-border and parcel delivery of pouches.

Reasoning:

  • Using the Porter screen as a disqualification test (Wisdom Library: "Five Forces is a disqualification screen"): rivalry, low; new entrants, low; substitutes (physical stores), high but losing share; buyer power, very low (fragmented consumers); supplier power, very high. One force out of five is hostile, and it is the strongest of them.
  • What keeps supplier power in check: brand owners earn more online (about 15% better margins), use Haypp to launch and test products, buy its data, and compete with each other for its visibility. A ten-brand-wide fight for consumers helps Haypp; a cartel-like calm or a single-brand-dominant market would hurt it.
  • Fragility points: (1) a supplier dispute; (2) an EU cross-border online ban (proposed by eight states, unlikely to pass quickly given 16 states have legal pouch frameworks); (3) carrier or locker delivery rules; (4) consolidation of brand owners lowering Media and Insights budgets.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?B605th of 24full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C+637th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the main substitute for buying from Haypp is buying the same pouch in a physical store, and that substitute is losing ground structurally; other substitutes (illicit products, vapes, caffeine pouches, synthetic nicotine analogues) are real but small and in several cases are being pushed back by regulators. The threat is moderate and slowly declining, except for illicit low-price products, which could depress price discipline.

Reasoning:

  • Physical retail: still the majority channel (over 90% of US pouches); offers immediacy and impulse purchase; loses on price (online 40 to 50% cheaper in the US, 20 to 30% in Scandinavia) and range (eight times the SKUs). Taxes and shelf constraints worsen its position.
  • Illicit products: about 15% of the US pouch market and about 70 to 75% of US vapes; illicit and unregulated pouches can disrupt price points. Enforcement (state directories, US Customs seizures) is improving but historically temporary.
  • Other nicotine formats: vapes face tightening rules (California single-use vape ban from January 2027; UK disposable ban); this pushes users toward pouches, which Haypp sells.
  • Non-nicotine: caffeine pouches (a small market forecast to grow from about $71m to $107m by 2032) are an assortment opportunity, not a threat.
  • Synthetic analogues (e.g. Nixodine): could escape weight-based taxes but face regulatory scrutiny; a multi-brand retailer can list them if legal.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

A721st of 248 answers
Judgement72C-4172C-4275C-4380C-4478C-4542C-4678C-4772
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A721st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Haypp's competitive advantage is real, multi-layered and mostly widening, but it is a distribution and data advantage, not a pricing or product advantage, and it sits inside a value chain whose brand power belongs to suppliers. Its strongest, mechanically compounding advantages are scale economies shared with customers (cheaper and broader than any alternative), organic customer acquisition, and the data and media position that turns its customer base into income from manufacturers. These give it near-certain dominance of the online channel and a long runway as the channel grows. Its weaknesses are the absence of consumer pricing power (margins are thin by design) and two dependencies (search platforms; manufacturers). For the second set: model durable revenue share gains and gross-margin support from Media and Insights mix, but not consumer-price-led margin expansion; treat AI-search-driven acquisition cost and supplier terms as the two key moat sensitivities.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A722nd of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Haypp holds four real advantages, of which two are strong and widening (scale-based procurement and fulfilment; the data and advertising position with manufacturers), one is strong but exposed to technological change (organic search authority, the low-cost customer acquisition engine), and one is moderate and regulation-dependent (compliance infrastructure). It holds no pricing-power moat and no production moat; the brand loyalty in the category belongs to manufacturers.

By type:

  • Customer-related, organic acquisition and retention (strong; at risk of narrowing): 97% organic traffic; 40% referrals; 90%+ repeat revenue; NPS about 80. Exposure: AI search and algorithm change. Early warning: a rising paid share of new customers.
  • Scale-based, procurement and fulfilment (strong; widening): 14 times the nearest rival; better buying terms; automated warehouses with spare capacity (Texas at one-third utilisation); fulfilment cost per can falling; these let Haypp offer 20 to 50% discounts profitably. This is "scale economies shared" (Wisdom Library): the retailer passes its cost advantage to customers, which widens the gap each period.
  • Data and advertising (strong; widening): Media and Insights about 12% of revenue, more than half of gross profit, growing about 40%; data leads offline by 12 to 18 months; manufacturers use it for launches. A "flywheel is not a moat" caution applies: the data is valuable only while manufacturers lack equivalent direct data, so this moat narrows if they build direct channels.
  • Regulatory and compliance (moderate; widening in the UK and Sweden; fragile in the EU): compliance raises entry costs (Wisdom Library: "state-granted moats are absolute and fragile"); but the same regulators could restrict the channel.
  • Culture and knowledge (moderate): management's industry relationships (BAT backgrounds), data platform built on AWS, fast adaptation (biometric lockers in weeks).
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A751st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Haypp is very large relative to retail rivals (about 10 to 14 times) and very small relative to its suppliers (PMI's revenue is about USD 40bn against Haypp's roughly SEK 4.8bn, under USD 0.5bn). Scale against rivals confers lower unit costs, better terms and the ability to absorb compliance and price investment; scale against suppliers confers relevance (it is their main online route) but not power.

Evidence:

  • 2026 revenue about SEK 4.8bn (consensus SEK 4.80bn); 682,000 active consumers a quarter; 1.45 million orders a quarter; 16 storefronts in seven markets plus Saudi Arabia; 304 full-time employees (Q3 2026, from 261 a year earlier).
  • Scale confers: procurement terms; fixed-cost absorption (G&A falling as a share of sales once the build-out ended); fulfilment automation economics (warehouse cost down from about 5% to 2% of order value); the ability to fund a SEK 39m quarterly marketing push from Core profits; the largest data set in online nicotine, which is the basis of Media and Insights.
  • What scale does not confer: price-setting power with consumers (Haypp follows manufacturer prices) or negotiating dominance with brand owners.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

A781st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Haypp's surplus customer utility is high and demonstrably not inferior for its target customer (the habitual, multi-brand, heavy user): it is cheaper, broader, increasingly fast, and well rated. It is inferior only on immediacy for light or impulse users, which is why its share of total pouch spend is still small in the US.

Evidence:

  • Price: 20 to 30% below stores in Scandinavia, 40 to 50% in the US and UK; at least 10% below manufacturer direct sites.
  • Range: about 3,500 SKUs (eight times a store); one-third of sales from products under a year old; manufacturers launch online first.
  • Service: NPS 80 to 82; same-day delivery pilots (Houston); about 40% of Stockholm orders delivered within 48 hours; lead times cut from about 20 to 4 hours; free UK shipping above £15; subscriptions with algorithmic flavour recommendations.
  • Disproof attempts: (1) the Swedish locker ruling adds friction at collection, a possible inferiority for locker users; (2) shipping costs and waiting times deter light users (Pacific Smoke expert); (3) during the ZYN pause, the offering was inferior for ZYN loyalists, and customers left. None disproves non-inferiority for the core customer today.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D4217th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: low. There is no evidence of Haypp applying pricing power to consumers; its prices follow manufacturers and taxes, and it deliberately gives scale benefits back as discounts. Its demonstrable "pricing power" is on the other side of the market: it charges manufacturers growing fees for media and data, which are effectively a price for access to its customers. That is real, applied and growing, but it is held at the suppliers' discretion.

Evidence:

  • Consumer side: Core price/mix positive only through pass-through; Growth price/mix negative; no disclosed widening of retail margin; the Reynolds American expert says brand-owner contracts limit online retailers' pricing.
  • Supplier side: Media and Insights from about 10% to 12.2% of revenue with gross margin far above product; it rose about 38.5% in Q2 2026 and about 41% (derived) in Q3; the 2025 ZYN restart agreement was structured through Media and Insights services.
  • Latent: a 40 to 50% US discount leaves room to narrow it (Deutsche Bank: Haypp can "quickly unwind consumer discounts"); this has not been used.
  • For the model: no consumer price-led margin expansion; model margin expansion through Media and Insights mix and operating leverage only.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

A781st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: to a high degree, and explicitly. "Scale economies shared" is the stated business model: purchasing scale and automation are converted into lower consumer prices and free or fast delivery, which accelerate volume, data and Media and Insights revenue, which fund further price investment. This is the strongest single source of Haypp's competitive durability and the reason its margin is structurally thin.

Evidence:

  • Management and Deutsche Bank describe a flywheel of procurement scale, lower prices, more volume, more data, more Media and Insights income reinvested in competitive pricing.
  • High-margin advertising revenue "allowed the group to fund competitive pricing for consumers without diluting underlying profitability" (Deutsche Bank, November 2026).
  • Behaviour matches words: the UK price and free-shipping offer; Q4 2026 reallocation of marketing to "the consumer proposition".
  • Implication for the model: the operating margin will stay low by design (target 4 to 7% EBIT in 2028); upside surprises in gross margin are likely to be partly reinvested, so the Consumer should not extrapolate gross-margin gains one-for-one into EBIT.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A721st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: to a high degree. Several mechanical forces improve Haypp's position each period without management heroics: users' rising daily consumption pushes them over the online threshold; a larger customer base makes Media and Insights more valuable; fixed infrastructure is filled; organic search authority compounds; compliance rules raise rivals' costs. The main thing that requires management choice is how much of the resulting gain is reinvested rather than retained.

Mechanisms:

  • Usage threshold: heavy users (seven to eight pouches a day) move online; US users report rising consumption.
  • Data network effect: more customers, more transaction data, more brand-owner spend, more price investment, more customers.
  • Fixed-cost absorption: Texas warehouse at one-third capacity; Milton Keynes quadrupled capacity with no staff increase; G&A flat in Q3 2026.
  • Search authority: a dominant site with 16 storefronts accumulates reviews, links and content (automated by HayppWriter).
  • Regulatory sieve: each new rule (UK age and ad rules, Swedish locker verification) removes weaker rivals.
  • Counter-force: the manufacturers' mechanical incentive to seek direct relationships grows as online's share grows (a balancing loop), and AI search could weaken the search-authority mechanism.

Management is a capable, ambitious, owner-aligned team that has built and defended the category leader through several external shocks, and that executes operational projects well; it is weaker at financial precision, cost discipline and compliance, and its communication withholds the two numbers that most affect EPS (marketing plans and the nature of recurring one-offs). The CEO's record says: trust the direction and the operational delivery, discount the timing of financial targets. Governance is adequate rather than prudent. For the second set: management's 2028 targets are a credible direction for revenue but, after the weak 2025, only the low end (SEK 7.1bn, needing about 22% growth a year in 2027 and 2028) looks achievable, and the margin target is a range, not a commitment; the track record of profit misses against consensus (at least three, probably four, of the last six reported quarters on the evidence available) argues for leaning 2027 EBIT below the bullish brokers and close to the Hold broker's slower path, and for not crediting management's informal optimism until the Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 reports show marketing intensity falling without a growth stall.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

A762nd of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the ambition is large and growth-first: to be the global online destination for nicotine pouches, doubling revenue from 2024 to 2028 and replicating a 36%-penetration Nordic online market in the US, the world's largest pouch market. It is bolder than any listed peer (manufacturers target 1 to 8% growth; Nordic e-commerce peers 12 to 19%), and it is backed by spending, not only words.

Evidence:

  • Targets: 18 to 25% constant-currency revenue CAGR 2024 to 2028; adjusted EBIT margin 5.5% plus or minus 1.5 points; three- to four-fold adjusted EBIT from 2024; US share of the total pouch market from 2 to 3% to 4 to 5%.

  • Peers: BAT 1 to 2% combustibles growth and 30% pouch CAGR inside a large mature group; Imperial low single digit; Apotea 15 to 19%. Haypp's ambition is closest to a category-killer e-commerce growth company.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

B685th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: the strategy is smart and well matched to the structure: own the online channel while it is small, before regulation (UK ad ban June 2027) and manufacturers close the window, and fund it from a mature cash engine; monetise the customer base through data sold to the suppliers; automate fulfilment ahead of volume. Its main flaw is that it widens a moat that sits inside the suppliers' power, so it is a strong but not dynamic strategy: it has few answers if a major manufacturer decides to go direct or restrict supply.

Reasoning:

  • Right priorities: US first (about 65% of incremental revenue), UK second (Europe's largest pouch market by 2030); scale economies shared; organic-first acquisition; compliance as a weapon; Media and Insights as both profit and supplier glue.
  • Bold, with correction: 2026 marketing push timed to the regulatory window and the return of ZYN; the Q3 2026 recognition that paid lower-funnel spend was partly wasted and the shift to price and product is the kind of adaptive correction a good strategy needs.
  • Gaps: (1) no visible plan B for a supplier withdrawal beyond range breadth; (2) Saudi Arabia is a single-brand market where Haypp's range advantage does not apply; (3) little evidence of strategic preparation for AI-mediated discovery beyond content automation; (4) "Flywheel is not a moat" (Wisdom Library): the data and price flywheel depends on manufacturers continuing to use Haypp as their neutral online partner.

Financial profile

D5510th of 248 answers
Judgement55C-4945C-5070C-5162C-5248C-5348C-5430C-7668
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D5510th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Haypp's economic engine is sound but its earnings are thin, managed and noisy. The engine: a fast-growing, asset-light, low-working-capital retailer whose gross margin has risen seven points in three years on the back of a high-margin media and data business, with a mature segment (Core) proving 12.5% EBITDA margins and a growth segment deliberately run at a loss. The weaknesses: operating margin of about 2%, EPS that has fallen while revenue accelerated, persistent "one-off" costs, capitalised development, an erratic tax rate, currency noise in finance costs, and weak free-cash-flow conversion on a full definition. For the second set, the key modelling insight is that consensus already equals "Core margin held at about 12%, Growth margin from -12% to about zero by 2028"; the forecast risk lies in (1) whether Growth revenue can keep growing about 40 to 50% a year (the consensus split implies about +48% in 2027) while marketing intensity falls, and (2) whether the Core margin of 12.5% (inflated in 2026 by price-rise and excise pass-through and stock gains) holds. Operating leverage makes 2027 EPS a wide cone (roughly 2.5 to 6.5 SEK on brokers' basis); the cuttable marketing line limits the downside to profit but at the expense of growth.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F4519th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Haypp is currently generating strong revenue growth (about 25% in 2026) with sharply falling EPS (brokers' adjusted EPS down about 45% in 2026, reported EPS negative for the first nine months), because profit is being reinvested. Historical EPS growth has been high but erratic and has never been durable for more than two years; the forecast rebound (+165% in 2027, +62% in 2028) is a margin-recovery call, not a growth extrapolation, and its durability depends on management's marketing choices.

Evidence:

  • Brokers' adjusted EPS: FY2023 2.48; FY2024 3.62 (+46%); FY2025 4.00 (+10%); FY2026E 2.19 (Deutsche Bank and FactSet median; Pareto 0.84, Barclays 2.91; -45%); FY2027E 5.80 median (mean 5.09); FY2028E 9.42 median (mean 9.06).
  • Reported basic EPS: FY2022 0.69; FY2023 0.17; FY2024 1.51; FY2025 1.39; nine months 2026 -1.25 (Q3 2026 alone -1.39). Reported EPS has never exceeded about 42% of adjusted EPS in the last three years.
  • Revenue growth in the same years: +22% (2023), +16% (2024), +5% (2025), about +25% (2026E). EPS growth has been inversely related to revenue growth in 2025 to 2026: when growth returned, management spent it.
  • Durability: the EPS path is a function of three discretionary or semi-discretionary items: marketing intensity, the Growth-segment cost base, and items affecting comparability. Two of three brokers model a near tripling of EPS in two years; the Hold broker models 0.84 to 3.58 to 8.02, a slower path that I regard as the more realistic shape for 2027.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

A701st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the scope is large and real: at least three to five years of customer acquisition in a US market where online is about 3% of a category growing about 15 to 20%, on infrastructure that is already built. The returns are attractive on plausible customer economics but not yet proven, because the "investment" is expensed marketing and personnel whose incrementality management itself questioned in Q3 2026. This supports an above-market multiple only once the Growth margin starts to improve.

Reasoning (illustrative, my estimates from company figures):

  • Customer economics in Growth. Annualised Q3 2026 Growth revenue (about SEK 1.56bn) over 227,000 active consumers is about SEK 6,900 per consumer per year; at a gross margin near 19% less about 2% fulfilment, contribution is roughly SEK 1,100 to 1,200 per consumer per year. Growth active consumers rose by about 80,000 year on year while Growth EBITDA swung from about breakeven (Q2 2025: +SEK 3.1m) to -SEK 48.4m a quarter, i.e., about SEK 200m a year of extra spend (marketing, US and UK organisations, Saudi). Even attributing all of that to new customers (it is not; ZYN's return also drove growth) gives a cost of about SEK 2,500 per added customer against SEK 1,100 to 1,200 of annual contribution and high retention (about 85% of second-order customers remain active): payback of roughly two years and a lifetime value two to three times acquisition cost. That is a good, not exceptional, return, and it falls if paid customers are partly ones who would have come organically.
  • Capital needs are low. Capex (fixed assets plus intangibles) SEK 106m (2024), SEK 90m (2025), SEK 96m (nine months 2026, of which SEK 68.7m software and SEK 27.4m warehouse equipment), expected to trend down; Texas can handle three to four times current US volume without new capex; working capital is low (22 days in 2025, falling).
  • Fair multiple. A three-to-five-year runway of 20%-plus revenue growth with a demonstrated mature-market margin (Core 12.5% EBITDA) justifies a premium to Nordic e-commerce (DNB Carnegie used about 12 to 15 times EV/EBITDA) and to tobacco manufacturers (6 to 10 times P/E), but only once the margin trajectory is confirmed; while EPS is being cut, the premium is borrowed from the future.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C628th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: returns on tangible capital are high (an asset-light, fast-turning retailer with low working capital), returns on total capital including acquisition goodwill are moderate and currently depressed by the investment phase, and incremental returns on new tangible capital are very high once volume fills the automated warehouses. The binding constraint on returns is margin, not capital.

Approximate figures (my estimates; the files do not give a full balance sheet):

  • Invested capital: consensus equity about SEK 656m for end-2026 (book value per share SEK 20.97) plus net debt of SEK 96m (all leases) gives roughly SEK 750m, a large part of it goodwill and intangibles from the Northerner and Scandinavian acquisitions and capitalised software. Tangible operating capital (net working capital SEK 169m on the company's measure, plus warehouse equipment and leased assets) is perhaps SEK 300 to 400m.
  • Returns: on FY2025 adjusted EBIT of SEK 150.7m (after about 22% tax, about SEK 118m), post-tax ROIC on total capital is in the mid-to-high teens and on tangible capital roughly 30 to 40%. On FY2026 adjusted EBIT of about SEK 105m both roughly fall by a third. Reported return on equity is single-digit (FY2025 net income SEK 42.5m).
  • Incremental: new tangible capital needs are small (tangible capex about SEK 27m in nine months of 2026); each additional SEK 1bn of revenue needs little working capital because payables fund inventory and customers pay at checkout (median three days' receivables). Deutsche Bank models ROIC reaching 62% by 2030.
  • Peers: Imperial 20.7%, BAT 9.0%, PMI about 45% ROIC; Haypp's tangible returns are comparable to the better peers, its total returns lower.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

D4816th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: on reported net income conversion looks very high (FactSet: 3.6 times in 2024, 3.1 times in 2025), but that is an artefact of depressed reported profit and of FactSet's definition, which ignores capitalised software and leases. On a full definition (after all capex and lease payments) against adjusted earnings, conversion has been poor and lumpy (roughly 58% in 2024 and under 20% in 2025 on my estimates), with working capital the main swing item. Working capital is structurally light and becomes a modest source of cash as the company grows.

Evidence:

  • FactSet FCF (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex): SEK 78m (2023), 160m (2024), 130m (2025).
  • Full FCF: after intangible capex (SEK 106m in 2024, 89.5m in 2025) and lease payments, Deutsche Bank puts FCF at SEK 63m (2024) and SEK 22m (2025); against brokers'-basis adjusted net income of about SEK 108m (2024) and 127m (2025) (my derivation: brokers' EPS times shares), conversion was about 58% and 17%. Free cash flow was negative in 2022 and 2023 (platform integrations).
  • 2026: nine-month operating cash flow SEK 212.8m (SEK 152.2m a year earlier), of which SEK 112.8m was a working-capital inflow (lower inventory after the late-2025 stock build ahead of price increases, and higher deferred revenue); capex SEK 97.6m.
  • Working capital: net working capital about 22 days in 2025 (30 in 2022), inventory turns about 11 times, receivables about three days, trade payables SEK 225.7m; Deutsche Bank expects the cash conversion cycle to fall to about nine days by 2030. Tactical inventory builds ahead of manufacturer price rises create quarter-to-quarter swings.
  • For the model: FCF is approximately adjusted EBITDA, less capex of about SEK 100 to 110m a year, less lease payments of about SEK 35 to 45m, less cash tax and interest, plus a small working-capital inflow with growth; it should turn clearly positive only when EBITDA exceeds about SEK 300m (2027 consensus SEK 332m).
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F4820th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the gross margin is low in absolute terms for a retailer but rising fast and structurally (12.7% in 2023 to a record 19.7% in Q3 2026), driven mostly by Media and Insights mix and better supplier terms; the operating margin is very low (adjusted EBIT 2.2% in Q3 2026) and has been falling in 2026 because almost all the gross-profit gain has been reinvested. The trend gap between the two margins is the central fact of Haypp's economics.

Evidence:

  • Gross margin: 12.7% (2023), 15.0% (2024), 19.2% (Q2 2025), 18.8% (Q3 2025), 19.5% (Q2 2026), 19.7% (Q3 2026); nine months 2026 19.3% against 18.8%. Gross profit grew 34.8% in Q3 2026 to SEK 241m, against revenue +28%.
  • Composition (derived): Media and Insights is about 12.2% of revenue and more than half of gross profit, so product gross margin is roughly 10 to 11% and Media and Insights gross margin above about 80%. Of the SEK 64m year-on-year increase in Q3 gross profit, roughly SEK 43m of extra Media and Insights revenue (from about 11.1% of SEK 952m to 12.2% of SEK 1,221m) could account for most of it.
  • Operating margins: adjusted EBIT 2.5% (2023), 3.7% (2024), 3.9% (2025), 2.3% (nine months 2026), 2.2% (Q3 2026); adjusted EBITDA 4.5% in Q3 2026 against 5.6%.
  • Segments: Core EBITDA 8.6% (Q2 2024), 10.2% (Q2 2025), 12.5% (Q3 2026); Growth EBITDA 1.5% (Q2 2025) to -12.4% (Q3 2026).
  • Trend outlook: gross margin flat to slightly up (Media and Insights mix up, Growth promotions and the ZYN 20-pouch can down); operating margin should trough in H2 2026 and recover as marketing falls. Consensus EBIT margin: about 1.9% (2026, on FactSet's basis), 3.4% (2027), 5.3% (2028).
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3018th of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean. The gap between adjusted and reported earnings is very large and persistent (reported EPS was 35% of brokers' adjusted EPS in 2025, 42% in 2024); "items affecting comparability" recur every year and include costs that a shareholder should treat as operating (legal settlements from the US business model, restructuring, executive bonuses); capitalised software flatters EBITDA; and currency revaluation and an erratic tax rate add non-operating noise to EPS. The Consumer should model a "recurring one-offs" line and a normalised tax rate.

Evidence:

  • Adjusted versus reported: FY2025 brokers' EPS 4.00 against reported 1.39; FY2024 3.62 against 1.51; FY2023 2.48 against 0.17. Q3 2026 adjusted EBIT +SEK 26.3m against reported EBIT -SEK 36.4m.
  • Items affecting comparability: SEK 30.4m (2024), SEK 52.6m (2025), SEK 52.7m (Q3 2026 alone, including the USD 2.5m legal provision and discretionary bonuses to let executives buy into the 2026 to 2029 incentive plan); an exceptional SEK 14.8m bonus in 2024 for the same purpose. US legal costs have now recurred in three consecutive years.
  • Capitalised costs: SEK 103.9m capitalised in 2025 and SEK 68.7m of software in nine months of 2026; amortisation sits below EBITDA. Brokers' EPS also excludes acquisition (purchase price allocation) amortisation.
  • Tax: reported rate 35.9% (2023), 23.7% (2024), 7.5% (2025), -11% (nine months 2026), because currency revaluations sit in other comprehensive income while their tax effects do not, and Growth losses in the US and UK may not create usable tax assets. A normalised 21 to 24% group rate is a reasonable modelling assumption once Growth is profitable.
  • Finance costs: SEK 5.2m (2024), SEK 12.5m (2025), SEK 12.2m (H1 2026), SEK 9.4m (Q3 2026), driven by unhedged revaluation of intercompany loans; Deutsche Bank's FY2026 EPS cut was entirely this.
  • For the model: carry SEK 25 to 40m a year of items affecting comparability through 2028 (legal, restructuring, incentive-related); model net finance cost of SEK 10 to 15m with a currency-revaluation risk band of plus or minus SEK 15m; use a 22 to 23% tax rate on adjusted pre-tax profit from 2027, with upside risk while the US loses money.
C-76

Judgement: financial leverage is immaterial and comfortable; it neither amplifies EPS nor adds risk. The only financial-line risk is volatility from unhedged currency revaluation of intercompany loans, which moves reported net finance costs by several SEK million a quarter.

Evidence:

  • Net debt SEK 96.3m at Q3 2026, entirely lease liabilities (SEK 88.8m non-current, SEK 33.4m current); net debt to LTM adjusted EBITDA 0.4x; cash SEK 174m; new SEK 400m revolving facility (August 2026) undrawn in substance.

  • For the model: no leverage-driven EPS effect; share count growing about 1% a year.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D459th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute almost nothing to group revenue growth in 2026 and 2027 and about 1.5 to 2 points a year from 2028, and almost nothing to margin directly at any point. Haypp is a price-taker on product: Core price/mix is pass-through of manufacturer list prices and Swedish excise, and Growth price/mix is negative on manufacturer promotions and ZYN's switch to 20-pouch cans. Its real pricing power sits on the supplier side, in Media and Insights fees, which I expect to grow about 30% in 2027 and 22 to 25% in 2028, from about 12% of revenue to about 14.5% by 2028. That is the de facto "price" line of the model and its largest single gross-profit driver. It is also the line most exposed to a shift in manufacturers' budgets. The latent consumer lever (narrowing the 40 to 50% US online discount) is worth about SEK 26m of 2028 EBITDA per percentage point of Growth price. I do not model it, because management's stated model is to share scale economies, not to harvest them.

Reasoning:

  • Core (Sweden, Norway): nominal, not real, pricing. 2026 Core growth of about 16% includes about 5 to 6 points of price/mix: manufacturer list-price rises passed through, Haypp's stock built ahead of them (sold at higher margin), and the January 2026 weight-based excise of SEK 209/kg. Core pouch volumes rose 14% and snus 1% in Q3 2026. The excise laps in January 2027, so Core price contribution falls to about 2 to 3 points a year (normal list increases) from 2027. The stock-build gains do not recur, which is one reason I hold the 2027 Core EBITDA margin at about 11.8% rather than Q3 2026's 12.5% (Deutsche Bank: 11.1% for 2026, 12.1% for 2027).
  • Growth (US, UK and others): negative price/mix through 2027. Manufacturer-funded promotions (BAT's Velo share gains, competitor US retail prices down 12% year on year in late 2025) cut Growth average order value 8% to SEK 785 and Growth gross margin to 18.9% from 24.4% in Q2 2026. ZYN's 20-pouch can (Q4 2026) cuts revenue per pouch by about 25% at a steady can price; customers buy fewer cans for the same use. This dilutes Growth revenue per pouch through Q3 2027, until the switch annualises. I model Growth price/mix at about -8 to -10% in 2026, -5% in 2027 and 0 to +2% from 2028. The turn depends on manufacturers seeking returns (Altria's on! +21.6% to USD 4.90 in Q3 2026 after cutting promotions; Jefferies expects two to three dominant makers to restore discipline; a Circle K category manager expects promotions to stay).
  • Group price contribution to product revenue. 2026: Core +5.5 × 68% ≈ +3.7 points; Growth -9 × 32% ≈ -2.9 points; net about +1 point. 2027: Core +2.5 × 64% ≈ +1.6; Growth -5 × 36% ≈ -1.8; net about 0. 2028 onward: Core +2.5 × 59% plus Growth +1 × 41%, about +1.5 to 2 points. Volume and mix do almost all of the work.
  • Margin effect of product price: approximately neutral. Haypp buys locally with fast inventory turns (about 11 times) and re-prices dynamically. Pass-through keeps the percentage margin and lifts absolute gross profit; promotions are manufacturer-funded and largely offset in cost of goods. Price matters for margin only through timing (stock gains before list rises, worth perhaps 0.5 to 1 point of Core margin in a quarter) and through Haypp's own choice of discount.
  • Media and Insights: the real price. It was 12.2% of revenue in Q3 2026 (11.1% a year earlier) and grew about 38 to 41%. Its revenue is about pure margin, and the September 2025 ZYN restart was structured as an agreement "that utilized Media and Insights services". The pricing is in annual calendar-year contracts (concluded in late 2025 for 2026), annual insight subscriptions, quarterly bespoke projects, multiyear US surveillance studies, sponsored listings, featured slots, and a sensory panel from 2027. My path: about SEK 570m (2026, 12.0% of revenue), about SEK 740m (2027, +30%, 13.4%), about SEK 925m (2028, +25%, 14.6%), then +20% and +17%. Each 10 points of Media and Insights growth in 2027 is about SEK 57m of gross profit, nearly a fifth of my 2027 EBITDA. Because management reinvests about half of the resulting gross-margin gain in consumer price, I let only about 0.3 points a year reach the group gross margin.
  • How each pricing source holds up across conditions. Manufacturer rivalry (now): good for Media and Insights, bad for Growth price/mix. Rivalry easing as the category consolidates (Advantage Solutions' October 2026 survey says nicotine manufacturers plan to cut trade-promotion spending by 47%; PMI moderated promotions from late-2025 peaks): Growth price/mix improves, but Media and Insights growth slows. These two partly offset, and that hedge is underappreciated. A supplier restricting supply (the ZYN pause precedent): both fall together. There is no hedge in that case, and Growth revenue fell 26% in Q2 2025. Excise and tax shocks: pass-through, plus faster online migration (New York's 75% wholesale tax adds about USD 2 a can). UK and Norwegian marketing and label bans: raise the value of Haypp's on-site media.
  • The latent consumer lever. Deutsche Bank argues Haypp can "quickly unwind consumer discounts"; a Reynolds American director says brand-owner contracts limit online retailers' pricing. The US discount (40 to 50%) leaves room. But the flywheel (lower price → volume → data → media income) depends on not using it, so I treat it as a scenario lever that would appear only if growth stalls.

For the model: no separate price line; revenue growth as in Part 1 already embeds the price/mix above; gross margin 19.4% (2026) rising about 0.2 to 0.3 points a year to 20.4% (2030).

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7562P-0164
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

A622nd of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: very material. With a contribution margin of roughly 10 to 18% of incremental revenue against a largely fixed G&A and depreciation base and a 2% EBIT margin, a 10% change in revenue moves adjusted EBIT by roughly 50 to 80% at current margins, unless marketing is flexed. Combined with the discretionary marketing dial and gross-margin sensitivity, this creates a very wide range of 2027 outcomes: on my estimates adjusted EBIT of roughly SEK 120 to 280m against consensus SEK 198m, or brokers'-basis EPS of roughly 2.5 to 6.5 SEK against a median of 5.80.

Reasoning:

  • Cost behaviour. Variable: cost of goods (product margin about 10 to 11%), fulfilment (about 1.7%), payment and parcel costs. Semi-fixed: G&A and personnel (about 10.4% of sales, flat in absolute terms Q2 to Q3 2026), depreciation and amortisation (about SEK 117m a year excluding acquisition amortisation). Discretionary: marketing (3.2% of sales in Q3 2026, about 1% a year earlier, under 0.5% historically).
  • Incremental margin. Deutsche Bank's sensitivities on 2027 imply about 10 to 11% incremental EBIT margin on revenue (1 percentage point of revenue growth equals SEK 0.15 of EPS) and show that 0.5 percentage points of EBIT margin equals SEK 0.74 of EPS (about 13% of 2027 consensus), 2 percentage points of volume equals SEK 0.53, and 5% currency equals SEK 0.18. With G&A truly fixed, the incremental margin on Core-like revenue approaches the contribution margin of about 17 to 18%.
  • Illustrative 2027 range: revenue plus or minus 5% (about SEK 290m) is plus or minus SEK 30 to 50m of EBIT; marketing plus or minus 0.5 percentage points of sales is plus or minus SEK 29m; gross margin plus or minus 0.5 points is plus or minus SEK 29m; G&A growth of 5% instead of 10% is plus SEK 25m. Taken together, roughly SEK 120 to 280m of adjusted EBIT.
  • Cushion: the marketing line (about SEK 150m a year at the 2026 run-rate) exceeds the whole of FY2026 adjusted EBIT, so management can protect EBIT in a revenue shortfall by cutting marketing, at a cost to future growth. This makes downside EBIT less severe than pure operating leverage implies, but makes the outcome dependent on management behaviour.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

A642nd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is the single largest driver of Haypp's profit over 2027 to 2030, and it is mainly G&A leverage, not fulfilment or marketing. The G&A and personnel base was built in 2025 and 2026 for a business much larger than today. If it grows about 7% a year while revenue grows 13 to 16%, it alone lifts the EBITDA margin by about 2.3 percentage points by 2030. On my base case, EBITDA grows about 40% a year in 2027 and 2028 on revenue growth of 15 to 16%, and adjusted EBIT grows 72% and 60%. That is a degree of operating leverage the company has never shown: from 2021 to 2025, margins rose only slowly while revenue compounded at about 14%. The leverage is real but back-end loaded. It also cuts both ways: each 1% of revenue missed costs about SEK 9m of 2027 EBITDA (about 3%), and each 1% of G&A overrun about SEK 5.5m.

Reasoning:

  • The cost structure (Q3 2026, company figures and my derivation). On revenue of SEK 1,221m: gross profit 19.7%; marketing 3.2% (discretionary); fulfilment 1.7% (variable, falling per unit); G&A about 10.4% (semi-fixed, flat in absolute terms Q2 to Q3 2026 after adjusting for holiday accruals); adjusted EBITDA 4.5%. Variable costs above gross profit are only about 2% of sales. The contribution margin on an extra krona of revenue is therefore about 16 to 18% before marketing, about four times the EBITDA margin. That ratio is the definition of high operating leverage.
  • Where the fixed base came from. G&A rose as the US and UK organisations were built (regional presidents, US legal and regulatory staff, people and culture, AI and central teams); headcount went from 261 to 304 in a year. Overheads rose to 12.4% of sales in FY2025 from 9.5% in FY2024. The build-out is now complete, on management's statement and on Deutsche Bank's reading of the flat Q3 G&A. My 2026 G&A is about SEK 508 to 515m (10.7% of sales). I grow it 8% in 2027 and about 7% a year after: wage inflation plus modest hiring, partly offset by AI tools (HayppWriter, automated replenishment). That takes G&A to 8.4% of sales by 2030.
  • Fulfilment and depreciation: leverage already banked, not ahead. Fulfilment is 1.7 to 1.8% of sales; cost per can fell 6% in Q3 2026 while volumes rose 22%. Texas runs at about one-third of capacity and Milton Keynes quadrupled its capacity with four core staff. But the Growth mix rising (Growth fulfilment is about twice Core's as a share of sales) offsets further unit-cost gains, so I hold fulfilment flat at 1.8%. Depreciation (excluding acquisition amortisation) rose from about SEK 86m (2025) to about SEK 110m (2026) as warehouses, the V3 storefront and new leases came into use. It grows only 5 to 8% a year from here (SEK 120m, 128m, 135m, 142m), so EBIT leverage exceeds EBITDA leverage. Like Apotea's Varberg and Lyko's Vansbro, Haypp's automation costs came first; the absorption is now ahead.
  • Line items against history. Adjusted EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis was 4.3% (2023), 4.9% (2024), 5.7% (2025), and is 4.6% in 2026. On the company basis, about 6.2% in 2025. Mine: 5.6% (2027), 6.8% (2028), 7.6% (2029), 8.4% (2030). Adjusted EBIT margin: 2.5%, 3.7%, 3.9% (2023 to 2025), 2.3% (2026), then 3.4%, 4.8%, 5.7%, 6.6%. 2028 is the first year above the company's historical peak, by about one point. The precedent that this is achievable inside the group is Core: its EBITDA margin rose from 8.6% (Q2 2024) to 12.5% (Q3 2026) as volume and Media and Insights filled a fixed base.
  • Why the leverage is back-end loaded and seasonal. Q1 carries the lowest margin (holiday accruals, Q1 revenue phasing). Q1 2027 still compares against Q1 2026, before most of the 2026 organisation was in place, so the year-on-year margin gain in Q1 2027 will be small (my 4.7% against about 4.8%). The visible recovery comes in Q2 and Q3 2027 (5.3% and 6.3% against 4.7% and 4.5%). The Consumer should not read a flat Q1 2027 margin (results around early May 2027) as failure of the leverage.
  • Width of the cone. In 2027, revenue ±5% (±SEK 275m) moves EBITDA by about ±SEK 45m (±15%); G&A growth of 12% instead of 8% costs SEK 21m; gross margin ±0.5 points is ±SEK 28m; marketing ±0.5 points is ±SEK 28m. Combined, my 2027 adjusted EBIT cone is about SEK 110 to 260m around a base of SEK 189m, close to the first set's SEK 120 to 280m. 2028 is wider (about SEK 180 to 400m) because the leverage compounds.
  • Peers. Chewy took its adjusted EBITDA margin from -6.5% (FY2018) to +3.0% (FY2022) and 6.8% (Q2 2026) on fixed-cost absorption plus sponsored ads; Zalando and Boozt show slower progress because their variable fulfilment and marketing costs are about 10% of sales or more each (Boozt fulfilment 10.8%, marketing 9.6% in Q3 2026). Haypp's variable costs above gross profit are about 4% of sales including marketing, so a larger share of each incremental gross-profit krona drops through. The constraint is not cost structure but the thin gross margin.

For the model: EBITDA margin 4.64% (2026), 5.6%, 6.8%, 7.6%, 8.4%; D&A SEK -110m, -120m, -128m, -135m, -142m. The operating leverage is the main reason my EPS more than triples from 2026 to 2028 even on revenue below consensus.

Financial leverage

C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C689th of 24full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

D588th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage will neither amplify nor drag on EPS. Haypp is in net cash excluding leases, and it moves to a large net cash position as free cash flow builds from 2028. The only financial-line risk is unhedged currency revaluation of intercompany balances, which moved net finance cost from SEK 5.2m (2024) to SEK 12.5m (2025) and about SEK 26m (2026E). That is noise of about ±SEK 0.3 to 0.5 of EPS a year, not leverage. The balance-sheet consequence that matters is an unused one: by end-2029 the company should hold net cash, excluding leases, of roughly SEK 0.5 to 0.6bn, and about SEK 0.9bn by end-2030. That is a capital-return lever (buybacks at a small-cap growth multiple) that no broker models and the company has not mooted. I keep it out of the base case.

Reasoning:

  • Starting point (30 September 2026). Cash SEK 174m; FactSet total debt SEK 270m, of which lease liabilities SEK 122.2m (SEK 88.8m non-current, SEK 33.4m current), so roughly SEK 148m appears drawn under the new SEK 400m Danske Bank revolving facility (August 2026). Reported net debt is SEK 96.3m (0.4x LTM adjusted EBITDA); excluding leases the company is about SEK 26m net cash. Note: the first set described net debt as "entirely leases". That is right in substance, but some bank debt is drawn and offset by cash.
  • Interest. Underlying cash interest is lease interest (about SEK 6 to 7m) plus facility costs (about SEK 3 to 7m, depending on drawings), partly offset by interest on cash. The rest of the reported net finance cost is revaluation of short-term intercompany balances through the income statement. Long-term intercompany revaluation goes through other comprehensive income, which also distorts the reported tax rate. I model net financial items of SEK -26m (2026, nine months already SEK -21.6m), then SEK -11m, -9m, -6m, -3m as debt is repaid and cash builds. I set currency at zero in the base, with a ±SEK 15m band each year.
  • EPS effect. Financial items take about SEK 0.25 off 2027 EPS and SEK 0.07 off 2030 EPS. Deutsche Bank's FY2026 EPS cut (August 2026) came entirely from this line, which shows that the line moves estimates, not value.
  • Share count. 31.8m (FactSet FY2025). The March 2026 warrant tender issued 502,402 shares for 775,000 warrants; I add 0.5m in 2026, but if FactSet's 31.8m already includes them the count is about 1.5% too high. LTIP 2026/2029 has up to 982,000 Class C2 hurdle shares with hurdles up to SEK 177.66; about 1.3% dilution at a SEK 300 share price on the company's illustration, conversion in 2029. I add 0.15m a year in 2027 and 2028 (other programmes), 0.4m in 2029 and 0.2m in 2030: about 1.2m shares (3.7%) over four years, about 0.9% a year of EPS dilution.
  • Balance sheet path. Cumulative full FCF of about SEK 870m from Q4 2026 to 2030, with no dividends, buybacks or acquisitions, takes net cash excluding leases to about SEK 0.9bn by end-2030, about 18% of today's market capitalisation (SEK 4.86bn). Lease liabilities rise modestly (UK warehouse, US office), to about SEK 140 to 160m. Equity rises by reported earnings; consensus has SEK 656m (2026) rising to SEK 1,327m (2029). Mine is a little lower because my reported earnings are lower.
  • Capital-return option. The board "does not expect to distribute dividends" and reinvests all free cash flow. With growth self-funding (capex about 1.5 to 2% of sales; working capital light), cash will start to pile up from 2028. A buyback of SEK 200m a year from 2029 at 20 times earnings would add about 1.5 to 2% a year to EPS. That is small but real, and it would change the equity story; the trigger would be the next capital markets day. Not in my base case; I flag it as upside.
  • Balance-sheet risk. Low: the facility is undrawn in substance, net debt to EBITDA is falling, and there are no covenants of concern in the files. The tail risk is not leverage but a supplier or regulatory shock that wipes out a year of FCF. The balance sheet can absorb that without equity issuance.
5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7772P-0352
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

A721st of 24

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: an unusually large share of Haypp's cost base above gross profit is cuttable quickly: marketing (about 3% of sales, of which at least two-thirds could be removed within a quarter, as the 2025 level of about 1% shows) and part of the Growth organisation's personnel and G&A. Cost of goods is not cuttable but its terms are negotiable; fulfilment is variable and already automated. In a downturn of volumes or a supplier shock, management could restore roughly 2 percentage points of margin within two to three quarters at the cost of growth.

By line (approximate shares of 2026 revenue):

  • Marketing (about 2.5 to 3.2%): fully discretionary; management already plans to cut lower-funnel paid spend in Q4 2026. Cutting to 1% of sales would add about SEK 100m a year to EBIT on 2026 revenue, roughly the size of FY2026 adjusted EBIT.
  • G&A and personnel (about 10%): partly cuttable; the US and UK organisations (Presidents, legal, regulatory, marketing teams) were built in 2025 to 2026; AI in central functions (HayppWriter, automated replenishment) should hold growth below revenue. Headcount 304 (Q3 2026) from 261.
  • Fulfilment (about 1.7%): variable and already low; further reductions come from volume density, not cuts.
  • Cost of goods (about 80%): set by manufacturers' prices; Haypp's discount to physical retail (20 to 50%) is its own choice and is, in effect, a cuttable "cost" of growth (narrowing it would raise gross margin).
  • New markets (Saudi Arabia): about SEK 4m a quarter; small, cuttable.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C526th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the announced cost action (the cut and redirection of lower-funnel paid marketing from Q4 2026) is real, but it is a reallocation more than an elimination. Management said the money will be "relocated" to the consumer offer and launches, so much of the saving will show up as price investment (lower gross margin), not as higher EBITDA. Against a counterfactual of 2027 marketing held at the Q3 2026 intensity of 3.2%, I estimate about SEK 55m of gross saving in 2027, of which about SEK 25 to 30m reaches EBITDA. Against the FY2026 average (2.25%), 2027 marketing intensity barely falls. Other identified eliminations are small: UK vape exit SEK 6 to 7m a year, already in the base; the forced reduction in UK paid marketing after the 1 June 2027 advertising ban, SEK 10 to 20m a year from H2 2027, likely partly reinvested; Snusnetto, negligible. What does reach the P&L is efficiency rather than elimination: G&A held to about 7 to 8% growth (AI, completed build-out) and flat fulfilment intensity despite Growth mix. The large cuttable reserve is a downside cushion: about SEK 100 to 120m a year (marketing to about 1%, Saudi Arabia paused, part of the Growth organisation), available within two to three quarters, but only at the cost of Growth revenue.

Reasoning:

  • What the incrementality finding implies. On the Q3 2026 call, management said its analysis showed that many customers acquired through paid lower-funnel channels would have found Haypp organically (97% of acquisition is organic). The cuttable share of marketing is therefore large; marketing was 1.0 to 1.1% of sales in 2025 and below 0.5% historically. But marketing was SEK 39m in Q3 2026 and about SEK 105m for the whole of 2026 (about 1.5%, 1.8%, 3.2% and 2.4% by quarter on my estimate). Q3 was a peak, not a base. My path: 2.4% (Q4 2026), 2.3% (Q1 2027), 2.4% (Q2 2027, pre-UK-ban push), 2.1% (H2 2027), then 2.0%, 1.9%, 1.8% (2028 to 2030). That is about SEK 121m in 2027 against SEK 177m at 3.2%.
  • Why half is reinvested. Management's words were "reduce and relocate" to the consumer proposition and launches. Its stated model is scale economies shared: the 1.7-point Media and Insights-driven gross-margin gain in Q1 2026 was "largely reinvested" in free shipping and lower prices. The UK free-shipping offer and the Growth gross margin of 18.9% (against 24.4%) in Q2 2026 show the pattern in action. So I model the gross margin rising only 0.3 points in 2027 (19.4% to 19.7%), although Media and Insights mix alone would add about 0.7 to 1.0 points.
  • UK advertising and sponsorship ban (1 June 2027). Haypp loses paid levers (including the PDC darts sponsorship) but acquires organically. The ban removes the main channel of paid-dependent rivals (Snus Vikings, Two Wombats) and should raise the value of Haypp's own on-site media to manufacturers. Net: a small saving in UK marketing from H2 2027 and a moat benefit, not a big P&L item.
  • Items affecting comparability will not be eliminated. SEK 30.4m (2024), SEK 52.6m (2025), SEK 52.7m in Q3 2026 alone: US legal provisions in three consecutive years, restructuring, consulting, and discretionary bonuses funding executives' incentive-plan participation. I carry SEK 30m (2027), SEK 25m (2028) and SEK 20m a year (2029 to 2030) of recurring items, all cash, plus payment of the USD 2.5m (about SEK 23.5m) provision in 2027. These sit below adjusted EBITDA, so they do not reach the adjusted lines, but they do reach reported EPS and FCF (P-08).
  • Timing. Q4 2026 (reported 10 February 2027) is the first quarter with reduced lower-funnel spend; I expect marketing at about 2.4% of sales and an EBITDA margin of about 4.6% (Q4 2025 about 5.3%). The cut therefore will not yet show as a year-on-year margin gain. The net efficiency becomes visible from Q2 to Q3 2027. The G&A leverage accumulates through 2028 to 2030.
  • The downside reserve, and the trade-off. Cutting marketing to about 1% of sales (the 2025 level) would add about SEK 65m to 2027 EBITDA. Pausing Saudi Arabia (about SEK 4m a quarter) would add about SEK 16m, and trimming the Growth organisation perhaps SEK 30m. That is about SEK 110m in total, a third of my 2027 EBITDA. This reserve is why a revenue shortfall would hit EPS less than pure operating leverage implies: management would cut, as it did in 2025 ("prudent on commercial spending"). But using it slows Growth, which postpones the 2028 targets. The cushion converts EPS risk into revenue-target risk.
  • Precedents. Lyko cut marketing 1.1 points to 8.5% of sales (Q2 2026) without hurting sales on its account, and earlier lost 4.8% of sales in Q1 2026 when it put profit first. Boozt cut 2.2 points (Q2 2026) and lifted its margin to 6.5%. Chewy's margin rose as marketing intensity fell, with a lag of a couple of quarters. The peers show that cuts from an inflated base do reach profit; Haypp's peculiarity is that it chooses to recycle them into price.

For the model: marketing as above; no restructuring savings line; recurring items affecting comparability as a separate cash line (Part 4).

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4772P-0560
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?A721st of 24full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B+603rd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: five reinforcing loops are operating now: procurement scale and price, data and media, organic search, the usage threshold, and the regulatory sieve. But four of the five are designed to widen volume and share, not margin, because management reinvests their gains in price. The loop that widens margin is a sixth one the brief does not name: fixed-cost absorption, where more volume fills the built G&A, warehouses and software. That is why I model revenue compounding at 13 to 16% with only a slowly rising gross margin, while EBITDA margin rises from G&A leverage. The balancing loops (supplier reaction, manufacturer consolidation, regulatory attention, AI search) do not show in the base case. They show as recurring costs (items affecting comparability of SEK 20 to 30m a year), as a slower Media and Insights growth rate after 2027, and as fat-tailed scenarios. The most consequential interaction is that the data-and-media loop and the supplier-reaction loop are the same relationship: the more manufacturers pay Haypp for access to its customers, the more they have an incentive to own those customers themselves.

Reasoning, loop by loop (what it does to the lines):

  • Procurement and price (reinforcing; strong; reinvested). About 14 times the nearest online rival and about 10 times Prilla. Better terms fund the 20 to 30% (Scandinavia) and 40 to 50% (US, UK) discount, which brings volume and the 7 to 8 pouches-a-day users. Financial effect: revenue and volume growth; gross margin flat, by design. Strength rises with each regulatory sieve and each failed rival (about 60 so far). What would stall it: a supplier restricting online terms (the Boozt precedent, where brands pushed back against online discounting, DNB Carnegie, November 2025).
  • Data and media (reinforcing; strong; partly reinvested). Haypp has a larger base, more transaction data (said to lead offline by 12 to 18 months) and more manufacturer spend; Media and Insights grew about twice the rate of group revenue since the IPO. Financial effect: the gross margin rose from 12.7% (2023) to 19.7% (Q3 2026), and Media and Insights is about 61% of gross profit. From here the loop slows mechanically as a share: growing at about twice a decelerating group rate, Media and Insights growth falls from about 40% to about 30% (2027) and 25% (2028). The new sensory panel and launch-testing products (2027) extend it. Peers support durability with a warning: Chewy's sponsored ads and Instacart's advertising (+23% in Q3 2026, 3.1% of transaction value) are the "predominant driver of improved profitability", but Instacart missed ad growth by 1.5% when a few large advertisers cut spend (late 2025). A concentrated advertiser base is the vulnerability, and Haypp's is three manufacturers.
  • Organic search (reinforcing; strong; under technological threat). 97% organic acquisition, 16 storefronts holding several top Google positions (the first three results take about 65% of clicks), a conversion rate of about 13%, and HayppWriter automating content. Financial effect: structurally low marketing (my 1.8 to 2.4% of sales), and the reason the incrementality finding (P-03) was possible. AI search recommends two to five sites (management, April 2025): this either entrenches the leader or shifts traffic to whatever an agent prefers. No data yet. The early warning in the numbers would be a rising marketing ratio without faster active-consumer growth.
  • Usage threshold (reinforcing; slow; the biggest long-run revenue driver). Users move online at 7 to 8 pouches a day; US average about 3.6 to 4. PMI research shows use rising from 5.2 pouches a day (under one year of tenure) to 7.4 (over three years). Financial effect: Growth revenue compounding at 22 to 34% a year through 2030 in my model, as the share of US users above the threshold rises. It is slow and mechanical: it cannot be accelerated much by marketing, which is why the 2026 paid push was partly non-incremental and why I do not expect Growth to keep 2026's 45% rate.
  • Regulatory sieve (reinforcing; episodic). UK age-18 rule (October 2026), UK advertising ban (June 2027), Swedish locker verification (October 2026; biometric deployed within weeks), US state product directories (about 18 states, about 49% of the population). Financial effect: share protection, plus higher compliance costs in G&A. These are part of why G&A is about 10% of sales and not lower, and why I let G&A grow 7 to 8% a year.
  • Fixed-cost absorption (reinforcing; the margin loop). Volume fills Texas (about one-third used), Milton Keynes (about half full), and the completed ERP, middleware and storefront platform; G&A as a share of sales falls. Financial effect: +2.3 points of EBITDA margin from 2026 to 2030 in my model, the largest component. This loop is weakened only if management re-expands the cost base (new countries, a new paid push) or if growth stalls.
  • Balancing: supplier reaction. A growing online share raises manufacturers' incentive to restrict terms, withdraw a brand or sell direct. Direct sites are about 1% of online volume today; BAT runs direct programmes elsewhere (it reaches 70% of adult nicotine consumers in Poland) but faces organisational limits. Financial effect in the base case: nil. In a scenario (10 to 15% a year), Growth revenue falls 20 to 30% for a year and Media and Insights falls with it; 2027 EBIT could halve. The dependence is now spread across two big brands (ZYN and Velo at about 28 to 30% US volume), a modest improvement since 2024.
  • Balancing: manufacturer consolidation and promotion fatigue. PMI's oral net revenue fell 4.4% organically in 2025 against prior-year promotion; the Advantage Solutions survey reports planned trade-promotion cuts of 47%. Financial effect: I slow Media and Insights growth to 25% in 2028 and 17% in 2030. A sharper slowdown (to about 10% in 2027) would cost about SEK 110m of gross profit, a third of 2027 EBITDA. That is the largest single downside sensitivity in the model and is under-discussed.
  • Balancing: regulatory and municipal enforcement against the visible leader. San Francisco settlement (SEK 18.1m, 2025); USD 2.5m provision for an unnamed city (Q3 2026); California and Massachusetts not served. Financial effect: recurring items affecting comparability (SEK 20 to 30m a year), carried below adjusted EBIT but in reported EPS and FCF.
  • Net interplay. The reinforcing loops make the revenue line unusually predictable in direction (habitual demand, more than 90% repeat revenue) and the margin line unusually dependent on two choices: how much of the media gain is reinvested in price, and how fast G&A grows. The balancing loops make the tails fat. This is why I lean the model to robust revenue growth with a slow gross-margin rise, and carry the supplier and Media and Insights risks as scenarios rather than averaging them into the base.

Haypp guides by ambition, not by commitment: long-dated, wide targets, reiterated each quarter, with no annual guidance and a history of re-dating rather than upgrading. On the 2028 horizon the targets sit above consensus on revenue and absolute EBIT; but because 2025 was lost, even the revenue floor now requires about 22% growth a year in 2027 and 2028, so the targets are not conservative, and only their low end looks achievable on current evidence. The margin target is credible at its low end because Core proves maturity economics, demanding at its midpoint. Informal commentary is more optimistic than both.
