Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C559th of 24

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Haypp fits the "category-killer online specialist in a structurally migrating staple category" pattern (outperformance comes when operating leverage arrives after an investment phase and is confirmed by upward EPS revisions) far better than it fits the tobacco-manufacturer pattern (outperformance from pricing plus buybacks at low multiples). Its own history shows that the shares re-rate on growth acceleration and de-rate sharply on regulatory or supplier scares and EPS cuts; today it sits in the middle of that pattern: growth confirmed, EPS still being cut, multiple already high.

Precedents and rules of thumb:

  • Tobacco manufacturers (Sector Digest): money is made from pouch scale (the biggest maker keeps the profit as price per pouch falls), buybacks at 6 to 10 times earnings, and stricter rules that the largest compliant maker carries best. None of these apply directly to Haypp, except the last, which applies at the retail level.
  • Nordic online retail (Boozt, Lyko, BHG, Apotea, RugVista): outperformance follows margin delivery after investment phases (Boozt's 2025 to 2026 earnings recovery; Apotea at 5.4% margin and Buy-rated); underperformance follows inventory or capex mistakes and demand cyclicality. Haypp's demand is non-cyclical and its inventory turns fast, so it avoids their two main failure modes; its failure modes are supplier and regulatory.
  • Haypp's own record: the share price nearly tripled in about eleven months to late 2024 and then fell on Swedish licence and supply news; the forward P/E compressed to about 10x in the worst regulatory panic (DNB Carnegie) and recovered as growth returned. Relative strength now: +4.9% over three months, +9.1% over twelve, 60th percentile of the universe.
  • Rules of thumb that fit: "Growth funded by incentives cannot fund margin" (promotion-funded US growth must prove itself when marketing falls); "Goldilocks growth" (20 to 30% revenue growth with rising margins is the sweet spot; Haypp has the growth but not yet the margins); "SS buy rating vs revisions divergence" and "composite signal masks component deterioration" (target prices up 25% in six months while FY2027 EPS was cut 20%): the market has paid ahead of earnings delivery.
  • What drives the next leg: a sustained turn from EPS cuts to EPS upgrades, which in this pattern needs two consecutive quarters of margin recovery without growth loss.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F3018th of 24

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: to a fair trough multiple alone, with forward earnings intact, the shares could fall about 40 to 48% (to about SEK 80 to 95). If the de-rating came with a 20 to 25% cut to forward EPS, which is how past troughs have happened, the fall would be about 55 to 60% (to about SEK 60 to 70). A severe supplier or regulatory event could take it further, toward DNB Carnegie's historical bear values of SEK 60 and, in an extreme licence-loss case, SEK 30.

Reasoning:

  • Starting point: price SEK 155.4 (11 November 2026); 24-month-forward consensus EPS SEK 8.05 (dashboard); 24-month-forward P/E 17.3x (FactSet, 6 November), at the 83rd percentile of five years and 87th of ten.
  • Own-history troughs: five-year 10th percentile 8.4x; 25th percentile 10.4x; the dashboard's ten-year bands: 8.7x "crash", 11.7x "conservative", 13.3x "prudent" (median); DNB Carnegie: the forward P/E fell to about 10x in the worst regulatory panic (2024 to 2025). The 52-week low was SEK 105.4 (32% below today).
  • Arithmetic: 8.05 times 10x is SEK 80.5 (-48%); times 11.7x is SEK 94 (-39%). With forward EPS 25% lower (about SEK 6.0): times 10x SEK 60 (-61%); times 11.7x SEK 71 (-55%).
  • Why a fair trough is about 10 to 12 times: that is where the multiple sat when growth paused and regulatory risk peaked, and it is still a premium to tobacco manufacturers (Imperial 5.9x, STG 7.0x, BAT 10.0x current P/E), justified by the growth runway. Below about 10x the shares would value Core alone at little more than a manufacturer multiple and the Growth option at nothing; that is the zone where a private-equity bid becomes plausible.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C588th of 24

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative staircase is one big step (Growth turning from heavy losses toward breakeven while still growing fast, provable between early 2027 and early 2029) surrounded by smaller, mixed regulatory and operational steps. The first step that can move estimates is close and dated (Q4 2026 results, 10 February 2027) and rests on an action management has already announced; but it coincides with a mechanical slowdown in reported growth as the ZYN base effect ends, so the first reading may be ambiguous. Regulatory steps are as likely to be read negatively (TPD3 draft, plain packaging) as positively (UK ad ban consolidation). With the multiple already at the 83rd to 87th percentile of its own history and EPS still being cut, the narrative needs the margin step to be delivered, not promised, before it can carry the shares further.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–62

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two sets are congruent on structure, direction and risk, and I found no contradiction that overturns the forecast. They jar in nine places. Three matter for the numbers (what actually has to change for profit to rise; the tax rate; the meaning of the Media and Insights path) and I resolve them here; one changes the inputs. Resolved, the forecast is more robust in its gross margin and less proven in its costs than either set says.

Where they align, and why that is worth something: both sets start from the same economic fact (a thin-margin retailer whose gross profit is mostly supplier-paid), reach the same frame for consensus (Core held at about 12% EBITDA margin, Growth from about -12% to about zero), the same hierarchy of confidence (revenue high, EBITDA moderate, earnings per share low), the same back-end loading, and the same two tail risks (supplier permission, permission to deliver remotely). The second set's model sits inside the first set's ranges on 2027 revenue growth (16.3% against 16 to 20%) and the 2028 margin (4.8% against 4.5 to 5.0%). An independent model landing inside an independent qualitative range is the main reason to trust the base case.

Where they jar, most important first:

  1. What has to change for profit to rise. The first set says the marketing dial is "the main determinant of 2027 EBIT"; the second says the recovery is mainly absorption of general and administrative costs, with marketing almost flat against the 2026 average (2.2% of sales against 2.25%). Both describe one requirement, visible when all costs between gross profit and EBITDA are taken together (my derivation from the sets' margins):
SEK m 2024 2025 2026E 2027E 2028E 2029E 2030E
Gross profit, change on the year +150 +175 +194 +167 +179 +188 +198
Operating costs above EBITDA, change +45 +148 +211 +78 +57 +71 +65
Adjusted EBITDA, change +105 +27 -17 +89 +122 +117 +133
Share of extra gross profit kept 70% 15% none 53% 68% 62% 67%
Extra EBITDA per krona of extra revenue 0.20 0.16 -0.02 0.12 0.15 0.13 0.15

The forecast asks nothing new of gross profit. It asks cost growth to fall by almost two-thirds, from SEK 211m to SEK 78m, and to stay there for four years. Of 2026's SEK 211m, marketing was about SEK 65m and general and administrative costs about SEK 130m; of 2027's SEK 78m, marketing is SEK 15m and general and administrative costs SEK 46m. So the first set is right that marketing must stop rising and the second is right that overhead absorption does the arithmetic; neither says plainly that the forecast is, in substance, a forecast of management's spending behaviour. A caution on the table: the step from 2024 to 2025 in both the gross margin (15.0% to about 18.9%) and the cost ratio (9.3% to 12.7% of sales) is so large and so symmetrical that part of it may be a change in what is classified above and below gross profit; I cannot verify that from the files. The EBITDA row and the last row are unaffected by classification. Over 2023 to 2026 as a whole each extra krona of revenue produced 0.07 of EBITDA; the forecast needs 0.12 in 2027 and 0.13 to 0.15 after.

  1. The Media and Insights path and the gross-margin input do not say the same thing, and the input is the more robust of the two. The second set describes Media and Insights growing 30% in 2027 and 25% in 2028 (to about SEK 740m and SEK 925m) and calls a slowdown to 10% "the largest single downside sensitivity", worth SEK 110m. But the model takes only the gross margin (19.4%, 19.7%, 20.0%, rising to 20.4%). Put together, the two imply that gross profit on products does not grow at all: about SEK 351m in 2026, SEK 348m in 2027, SEK 342m in 2028, on product revenue rising 29%, so that the product margin falls from 8.4% to 7.3% to 6.3% (and 5.8% by 2030). The company's own split says otherwise so far: product gross profit was SEK 93m in Q3 2026 against SEK 73m a year earlier (+27%), a flat margin of about 8.7%. The falling product margin is the second set's allowance for management giving Media and Insights gains back to customers in price. It is a cushion: if the product margin merely holds, the 2027 gross margin is met with Media and Insights growing about 20%, not 30%; and by 2030 the 20.4% input needs Media and Insights at only about 13% of revenue, not 15.5%. I therefore disagree with the size of the second set's sensitivity. A shortfall in Media and Insights and the price reinvestment are two sides of one management decision: with less supplier income to give away, less is given away. Like the marketing line, this turns a profit risk into a growth risk. The exception is a supplier withdrawing a brand, when revenue and Media and Insights fall together.

  2. The tax rate is inconsistent with the nine-month accounts and with the second set's own Growth forecast. For 2026 the input is 28% of adjusted pre-tax profit. The nine months show a reported pre-tax loss of about SEK 35m and a net loss of about SEK 38.7m, a tax charge of about SEK 3.7m, against adjusted pre-tax profit of about SEK 60m: about 6%. Reaching 28% for the year would need about SEK 20m of tax in the fourth quarter on about SEK 24m of adjusted pre-tax profit. The model's own history has the same pattern: tax on its definition of pre-tax profit was 11%, 16% and 3.5% in 2023 to 2025. The rate on adjusted profit is low for a structural reason the second set did not use: items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation reduce taxable profit or release deferred tax, but are excluded from adjusted profit. It is pushed up by losses in the United States and United Kingdom companies that earn no relief (no deferred tax asset is recognised; confirmed by the research report on tax), and moved either way by currency effects on loans within the group, which are taxed but booked outside the income statement. My estimate of the net of these is about 24 to 27% in 2027 (unrelieved losses at their peak relative to profit), about 21 to 23% in 2028 and below 20% from 2029, when the second set's own forecast has Growth in profit (EBITDA of SEK +58m, then +162m) and the United States and United Kingdom profits are sheltered by losses never booked as an asset. The second set named this as "upside" but kept the rate above the Swedish 20.6% to 2030, which contradicts its own segment path. I change the line to 15%, 25%, 23%, 21%, 20%, still above my structural estimate in the later years.

  3. Free cash flow has three values for 2027 (first set: "clearly positive" above SEK 300m of EBITDA; second set SEK 49m; model SEK 91.5m). Resolved above: about SEK 43m to 49m.

  4. The first set's reading that estimates have been "cut deeply and broadly" does not hold for the broker that sets the median. See I-02: the 2027 reset has happened at Pareto only.

  5. Core's record margin. The first set attributes Core's 12.5% margin partly to price pass-through and stock gains; the second holds Core at 11.8% in 2027, rising to 12.7%. My derivation in D-02 shows that the SEK 39m rise in Core EBITDA in Q3 2026 is almost exactly the rise in Core's Media and Insights income (about SEK 35m). The Core margin path therefore depends on Nordic suppliers' spending growing faster than Core revenue, the same dependency as the rest of the forecast, not a separate, safer one.

  6. Growth price and mix. Both sets hold it negative to mid-2027. Growth's average order value rose 3.3% in Q3 2026 (SEK 847 against SEK 819) after falling 8% in Q2, in Swedish kronor and with a weaker dollar. The assumption was already too negative before ZYN's change of can size, which now pushes the other way; the two roughly offset and I leave revenue unchanged.

  7. The probability of a severe event. The first set puts at 15 to 25% the chance of at least one major supplier or regulatory event in 18 months; the second set's scenarios give the tail 10%. I use 12% for an event that damages 2027 earnings, since not every event lands in that year (D-04).

  8. The 2028 revenue target. The first set expects SEK 6.6bn to 7.0bn, the second SEK 6.34bn, "11% below" the floor. The target is at constant currency from 2024; currency has taken about 2.5 points off reported revenue since then, so the floor in today's kronor is about SEK 6.95bn and our forecast is about 9% below it. I side with the second set's level, because it is built from the quarterly run-rate.

One assertion in the second set I could not confirm: that a survey by Advantage Solutions in October 2026 found nicotine manufacturers planning to cut trade promotion by 47%. The research commissioned to test it found no such survey, only that Advantage Solutions' own branded-services revenue fell and that consumer-goods budgets are under scrutiny; it found the opposite for 2027 in nicotine (D-03). The bear case should not rest on that datum.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

–36

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the structure of the case is fully articulated, by one broker, and favourably; the quantities that decide the next 18 months are not articulated by anyone. Deutsche Bank's research sets out the channel migration, the supplier-paid income, the unpaid customer acquisition, regulation as a barrier and the Growth margin path, and treats most of what we count as risk as strength. Not in any commentary the files show: that the recovery is a matter of cost behaviour; how large the growth slowdown is; that gross profit on products is flat in our forecast; where the supplier income is earned; the tax, one-off and share-count conventions beneath "adjusted" earnings; the gap between adjusted earnings and cash; and what the multiple becomes on lower estimates. For investor commentary the files hold no evidence at all, so what investors know is my inference, held with low confidence.

The evidence and its limits. The research report on the narrative draws on Deutsche Bank (Buy; initiation "A Pure Nicotin-E-Pouch Play", 9 April 2026; "Growth Opportunity Hard-to-Miss", 6 November 2026; several notes a quarter), on DNB Carnegie up to its last note (Hold; coverage ended 26 June 2026; a bear value of SEK 60), and on Jefferies and Morgan Stanley, which write about Haypp as evidence for Philip Morris and British American Tobacco. It holds no document from Barclays or Pareto, two of the three brokers on FactSet, and it returned nothing from X, Swedish investor forums or media, or microcap newsletters, although asked. "Sell-side narrative" below therefore means Deutsche Bank's, with DNB Carnegie's earlier caution. Pareto's numbers show that a sceptical view exists; its reasoning is unknown to us.

Each dynamic, by how well it is articulated:

Well known, in print for a year or more.

  • The move of heavy users online (about 3% of United States pouch sales online against 36% in Sweden; the threshold of seven to eight pouches a day). Deutsche Bank, Jefferies and Morgan Stanley. This is the bull case, including Deutsche Bank's calculation that doubling United States online penetration would lift 2028 EBITDA by 75 to 150%.
  • The Growth margin path (-12.4% to -4.5% to +0.3%). The central debate, in Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie.
  • Suppliers' power to withdraw supply. DNB Carnegie called it the primary risk in almost every note; Deutsche Bank calls it muted because online is cheaper for manufacturers. Known as a risk; nobody attaches a probability.
  • Media and Insights as more than half of gross profit (61%). Deutsche Bank puts it at the centre of the case, as a barrier and a flywheel the market "underappreciates". The concentration of payers appears in DNB Carnegie and as a caveat in Deutsche Bank. Nobody has written that the retail operation would lose about SEK 90m a quarter without it, that it supplies almost all the forecast growth in gross profit, or how it divides between Core and Growth.
  • Regulation as a barrier, and the United Kingdom advertising ban. Deutsche Bank at length. The European proposal: a threat in DNB Carnegie, "highly unlikely" to become a ban in Deutsche Bank.
  • ZYN's larger can. Thoroughly covered as a Philip Morris matter (25% less revenue per pouch; a drag to Q3 2027). For Haypp there is only the company's remark that volumes counted in cans will be affected; nobody has sized it. On my estimate it costs about 2% of revenue and very little profit, so the risk is a false alarm.

Known in kind, not in size.

  • The slowdown as the ZYN return is lapped. Deutsche Bank expected it to "slightly temper" growth; DNB Carnegie noted in March 2026 that underlying growth had slowed in 2025. The two brokers' numbers imply 23% growth in Q4 2026; ours is 18.6%, with Growth falling from 54% to about 28%. The size is not in the narrative, so a fall of that size is likely to be read first as weaker demand.
  • The marketing cut as a reallocation. Deutsche Bank says plainly that it is an optimisation redirected into the customer offer and launches, and lowered its 2027 Growth margin. Nobody has said that 2027 marketing is about level with the 2026 average (2.2% of sales against 2.25%), so that the cut restores a run-rate rather than adding margin.
  • AI-mediated search against 97% unpaid traffic. Deutsche Bank argues the position is defensible; the company lists it as a risk; sector notes cite the share of searches that end without a click. Discussed without data.
  • Cash against adjusted earnings. Deutsche Bank records that free cash flow conversion fell from 61% to 20% in 2025 and expects capital intensity to fall. It is not a point of debate.

Barely or not articulated.

  • Cost behaviour as the thesis: costs above EBITDA up SEK 211m in 2026 and required to rise by SEK 78m in 2027.
  • The tax rate while the United States and United Kingdom lose money. Deutsche Bank normalises at 20.6%; nobody discusses the rate in the loss-making years, or the shelter when Growth turns to profit.
  • Recurring items affecting comparability, including cash bonuses that fund executives' participation in the incentive plan. Disclosed by the company; no broker makes a point of it; every adjusted figure excludes it.
  • Flat gross profit on products; Core's margin as supplier income; supplier income in Growth lagging the customer base. My derivations; not in print.
  • The Growth customer spending as much as the Core customer. Also mine, and a positive that is not in print either.
  • Manufacturers' budgets as a risk to Media and Insights. Not a topic, and the near-term evidence points the other way.
  • Capital returns. Nobody; Deutsche Bank models none; the company's policy is to reinvest everything. On my adjustment of the model, net cash of about SEK 700m by 2030 would be about 14% of today's market value.
  • The 2028 arithmetic: revenue at the floor only on the bullish broker's numbers; an EBIT ambition above every forecast.
  • The multiple on lower estimates: 17.3 times on consensus, 24.6 times on ours.

Investors. No direct evidence. What can be inferred: the register is concentrated (founders and venture funds, Fidelity at 9.9%, Robotti at 5.4%); many Nordic funds cannot hold the shares; the company presents at United States microcap conferences; about USD 0.5m of shares trade a day; one broker publishes regularly. In that setting the investor narrative is most likely the company's and Deutsche Bank's, and the unarticulated items are unlikely to be widely understood. DNB Carnegie's observation that the shares react to near-term earnings and regulatory headlines fits a holder base that follows the story more than the model.

What a disappointment or a surprise will be measured against: Deutsche Bank's 2027 (revenue SEK 5,863m, EBITDA SEK 354m, earnings per share 5.80, Growth margin -4.5%) and its SEK 220 target. The balance of the unarticulated items is negative for the next six to twelve months (the size of the slowdown, the earnings conventions, the multiple on lower estimates) and positive only further out (customer value, the catch-up of supplier income in Growth, the tax shelter, capital returns).

Secondarily, 18 months to three years: I expect attention to move to 2030 targets, to the first discussion of what to do with cash once Growth funds itself, to the European rules as their application date (2029 to 2030) approaches, to the United Kingdom's approval scheme (from April 2028), and to whether AI-mediated search has changed the cost of acquiring customers. None of these is priced as a debate today.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 12 November 2026, 04:37 to 05:02 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Haypp Group AB (HAYPP-SE): First set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-12 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst answering the second set (operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before the answers: what the Consumer needs to know first

Source check. The "latest Earnings Review" (file 06) does cover the results for the period ending 30 September 2026: it reviews the Q3 2026 interim report published on 5 to 6 November 2026 (sources give both dates). It can be relied on. The "earlier" Earnings Review (file 07) covers Q2 2025, not Q2 2026; it is useful mainly as the trough-of-the-ZYN-shortage baseline.

The one-paragraph frame for the whole set. Haypp is two businesses inside one P&L, and its group operating profit is a residual that management sets. (1) Core (Sweden and Norway): about SEK 3.3bn of 2026 revenue, a record 12.5% EBITDA margin in Q3 2026 (9.2% a year earlier), mature, cash-generative. (2) Growth (US, UK, Germany, Switzerland, now Saudi Arabia): about SEK 1.45bn of 2026 revenue growing 50%+, run at a deliberate -12.4% EBITDA margin in Q3 2026 because marketing was lifted to 3.2% of group sales (1.0% a year earlier). Underneath both sits a gross-profit structure that the market under-describes: Media and Insights (retail media and consumer data sold to the manufacturers) is 12.2% of revenue but, on the company's and Deutsche Bank's statements, more than half of group gross profit. My derivation from those statements: the product-retail gross margin is roughly 10 to 11%, and Media and Insights carries a gross margin above about 80%. Haypp is therefore best modelled as a thin-margin online retailer whose profit is mostly a retail-media business paid for by its own suppliers. That one fact explains the moat, the main risk, and most of the earnings sensitivity.

Data traps the Consumer must avoid (each checked against the files).

  • FactSet's "gross profit" and "gross margin" lines in the financials block (2.8% gross margin for FY2025) are not the company's gross margin; they replicate EBIT. The company's own gross margin was 12.7% (FY2023), 15.0% (FY2024), 19.2% (Q2 2025), 19.5% (Q2 2026) and 19.7% (Q3 2026, a record). Use the company's figures.
  • Consensus is thin: Deutsche Bank (Buy, SEK 220), Barclays (Overweight, SEK 210) and Pareto Securities (Hold, SEK 160) are the brokers FactSet shows; revenue and EBITDA rest on two of them. The research reports describe Deutsche Bank as the "sole active" broker after DNB Carnegie dropped coverage on 26 June 2026; that is wrong per FactSet. Write "two or three brokers expect", not "consensus expects".
  • FactSet consensus net income for FY2026 (-24.8m) and consensus adjusted EPS (2.19) sit on different bases; the brokers' adjusted EPS excludes items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation. Never compute growth from history lines to consensus lines; use the block's like-for-like line (FY2025 base on the brokers' basis: revenue 3,899, EBITDA 221, EPS 4.00).
  • Reported net debt of SEK 96.3m (Q3 2026) is entirely lease liabilities; ex-leases the company is in net cash.
  • The dashboard's "buy share 33%" conflicts with FactSet's two Buys out of three named brokers; treat buy-share as unreliable.
  • US online share is cited as 85% (Deutsche Bank, 2026) and as 55% (DNB Carnegie, November 2025); the sources disagree.

Fiscal year is the calendar year. Next results: 10 February 2027 (Q4 2026).

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Notes the Investment Analyst wrote inside its groups

Inside the group Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)

A framing point for this whole group: Haypp competes in two markets at once. In online nicotine retail it is the dominant number one. In total nicotine-pouch retail (stores plus online) it is a small player (by its own statement 2 to 3% of the US pouch market in 2024, targeting 4 to 5% by 2028). Its real competitor is the physical store, and its real counterparty power problem is upstream (three manufacturers), not among retail rivals. The answers below keep these two lenses separate.

Inside the group Company - Economic strength (Layer 1)

A modelling note for the whole group. My decomposition of the cost structure from Q3 2026 company figures (revenue SEK 1,220.9m): gross profit SEK 241m (19.7%); marketing SEK 39m (3.2%); fulfilment about 1.7% of sales (about SEK 21m); G&A about 10.4% of sales (about SEK 127m); adjusted EBITDA SEK 55.5m (4.5%); depreciation and amortisation (excluding acquisition amortisation) about SEK 29m; adjusted EBIT SEK 26.3m (2.2%). Segment EBITDA: Core SEK 104m (12.5% of SEK 832m), Growth -SEK 48.4m (-12.4% of SEK 389m); the two sum to group adjusted EBITDA, so central costs are allocated. Consensus reconciles to this structure: FY2027 EBITDA of SEK 332m is what you get from Core (SEK 3.58bn at about 12%, about SEK 430m) plus Growth (SEK 2.17bn at Deutsche Bank's -4.5%, about -SEK 97m); FY2028 EBITDA of SEK 499m is roughly Core SEK 3.76bn at 12.4% (about SEK 466m) plus Growth SEK 2.98bn at about breakeven. So consensus is, in effect, "Core margin held at about 12%, Growth margin from -12% (2026) to about -4.5% (2027) to about zero (2028)". That is the frame for every answer below.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Haypp Group AB (HAYPP-SE), a Stockholm-listed online retailer of nicotine pouches and snus (storefronts including Snusbolaget, Nicokick and Northerner), reporting two segments, Core Markets (Sweden and Norway) and Growth Markets (United States, United Kingdom, Germany, Switzerland and, from July 2026, Saudi Arabia), plus a Media and Insights business that sells advertising, launch services and consumer data to nicotine manufacturers.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Boozt (Nordic online retailer that went through marketing-led investment phases and margin recovery); Apotea (Swedish online pharmacy with an automated warehouse and a stated margin progression); Lyko and BHG Group (Nordic online specialists with long margin histories through up- and down-cycles); Chewy (United States online category specialist with repeat-purchase customers that moved from heavy losses to profit as marketing intensity fell); Zalando (European online retailer that cut paid marketing and grew its advertising business); Prilla (private United States online nicotine retailer, Haypp's main direct United States competitor); Snus Vikings and Two Wombats (private United Kingdom online nicotine retailers). Inputs: Which parts of Haypp's cost base above gross profit are fixed (general and administrative costs, personnel in the new United States and United Kingdom organisations, technology, depreciation of software and automated warehouses), which are variable (fulfilment, shipping, payment costs) and which are discretionary (marketing), using company disclosures such as marketing at 3.2% of net sales in Q3 2026 against 1.0% in Q3 2025 and fulfilment at 1.7% of net sales in Q3 2026 (Q3 2026 report, November 2026)? How did the Core Markets EBITDA margin move with volume and Media and Insights growth (company figures: 8.6% in Q2 2024, 10.2% in Q2 2025, 12.5% in Q3 2026), and what drove each step? What did the Growth Markets segment's EBITDA margin do when marketing was raised in 2026 (company figure: -12.4% in Q3 2026), and what happened at the peers when they cut paid customer acquisition after an investment phase: how quickly margins recovered, and whether customer growth slowed? What does management's statement on the Q3 2026 call that many customers acquired through paid lower-funnel channels would have found the platform organically imply for the cuttable share of marketing, and what precedents exist for such incrementality findings? How much spare capacity is there in the automated Texas warehouse (described by Deutsche Bank as about one-third utilised) and in Milton Keynes (capacity quadrupled in 2026), and what have comparable automation programmes delivered in cost per order against what was promised? Which of the items affecting comparability (SEK 30.4 million in FY2024, SEK 52.6 million in FY2025, SEK 52.7 million in Q3 2026, including legal provisions, restructuring and discretionary bonuses) are likely to recur? How will the United Kingdom advertising and sponsorship ban from 1 June 2027 change Haypp's marketing costs and those of its United Kingdom competitors? What costs sit behind the Media and Insights revenue, and what is its incremental margin? Focus: How much of Haypp's 2026 operating-margin compression reverses mechanically as marketing intensity falls and fixed costs are spread over higher volumes, and how fast the Growth Markets segment can move toward the Core Markets cost structure given that United States customers use far fewer pouches a day than Scandinavian customers.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Philip Morris International (owner of ZYN, Haypp's largest supplier brand, which paused online sales in the United States in 2024 to 2025 and moved to 20-pouch cans in Q4 2026); British American Tobacco (owner of Velo, gaining United States share through promotions); Altria (owner of on!, which raised its can price 21.6% in Q3 2026); Imperial Brands (Zone at discount prices; bought Helwit in September 2026); Scandinavian Tobacco Group (XQS; formerly operated zyn.com); Chewy and Zalando (online specialists that sell advertising to their suppliers); Maplebear (Instacart) (retail media priced to consumer-goods brands); Prilla (private United States online nicotine retailer). Operating conditions: manufacturer promotion wars in the United States pouch category (2025 to 2026); a supplier withdrawing or restricting a leading brand from the online channel (the ZYN pause, 2024 to 2025); manufacturer list-price increases; excise and tax shocks (Sweden's weight-based pouch excise from January 2026; United States state pouch taxes such as New York's 75% wholesale tax); marketing and labelling restrictions (United Kingdom advertising ban from June 2027; Norway's ban on flavour and strength descriptors from January 2027); consumer budget pressure and down-trading; changes in can size and pouch count. Inputs: Does Haypp set its own consumer prices, or do they follow manufacturers' list prices and contractual pricing controls, and how far can it narrow its discount to physical retail (described as 20% to 30% in Scandinavia and 40% to 50% in the United States and United Kingdom by the company and Deutsche Bank, April 2026) without losing customers? How did Haypp's price/mix and gross margin behave under manufacturer promotions (Growth Markets gross margin 18.9% against 24.4% a year earlier and average order value down 8%, Q2 2026 interim report, 12 August 2026)? How is Media and Insights revenue priced (campaign fees, licence fees for data, launch packages), how often are terms renegotiated (annual negotiations concluded in late 2025 for 2026, per the Q4 2025 call), and how does it behave when manufacturers compete hard against when they cut trade-marketing budgets? Was the September 2025 ZYN restart "mutually beneficial agreement that utilized Media and Insights services" (Q3 2025 call) a sign that supplier access is partly paid for through Media and Insights? How do peers' retail-media businesses hold up in supplier downturns? What do manufacturers, competitors and the sell-side say now about online retailers' pricing power and about manufacturers' minimum-price policies? Focus: Whether Haypp's real pricing power lies with consumers (narrowing its discount) or with suppliers (Media and Insights fees), and how each holds up when manufacturer rivalry eases, a supplier restricts supply, or taxes and marketing rules change.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Haypp's margins and growth are set by interlocking loops (scale, price, customers, data and supplier income reinforcing one another, against supplier and regulatory reactions that grow with its online share), so the second set needs to know which loops are strengthening and which could reverse. Peers: Chewy and Zalando (online category specialists whose scale, retention and advertising loops have been tested over many years); Boozt (Nordic online retailer with a scale and logistics loop); Philip Morris International and British American Tobacco (the suppliers whose incentives to sell through or around Haypp are the main balancing force); Maplebear (Instacart) (retail media funded by consumer-goods brands); Prilla (private United States online nicotine retailer). Inputs: The procurement and price loop: does greater purchasing scale give Haypp better terms that it passes on as lower prices, which bring more volume (about 14 times its nearest online competitor, per Deutsche Bank, April 2026)? The data and advertising loop: does a larger customer base make Media and Insights more valuable to manufacturers (12.2% of net sales in Q3 2026 against 11.1% a year earlier, company), and is that income reinvested in prices? The organic search loop: does a dominant site attract more reviews, content and links, keeping about 97% of customer acquisition organic, and how is AI-driven search changing this? The usage-threshold loop: do United States users move online as daily consumption rises toward the seven to eight pouches a day at which users switch online (Jefferies, July 2026)? The regulatory sieve: do stricter rules (United Kingdom age and advertising rules, Sweden's parcel-locker verification ruling of October 2026, United States state directories) push share toward the compliant leader? The balancing loops: does a growing online share raise manufacturers' incentive to sell direct, restrict online terms or withdraw brands (the 2024 to 2025 ZYN pause); does manufacturer rivalry that funds promotions and Media and Insights fade as the category consolidates; does Haypp's growing visibility attract more municipal and state enforcement (United States provisions of USD 2.5 million in October 2025 and November 2026)? Focus: Which reinforcing loops are now strong enough to widen Haypp's margin rather than be reinvested in price, and which balancing loops (supplier reaction, manufacturer consolidation, regulatory attention, AI search) could stall or reverse them over the next 18 months to four years.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Haypp's adjusted EBIT margin was 2.2% in Q3 2026 (company) and its reported EPS differs widely from the brokers' adjusted EPS (FY2025: SEK 1.39 reported against SEK 4.00 on the brokers' basis, FactSet), so each input between gross profit and EPS needs its own evidence. Peers: Boozt, Apotea and Lyko (Nordic online retailers with comparable tax, currency and adjusted-earnings reporting); Chewy (online specialist with a long gap between adjusted and reported profit during its investment phase); Scandinavian Tobacco Group and Imperial Brands (Nordic and European nicotine companies with currency translation and regulatory-cost exposure). Inputs: The path of marketing as a share of net sales after the Q4 2026 reduction in lower-funnel spending announced on the Q3 2026 call; the Growth Markets EBITDA margin path (Deutsche Bank models -4.5% for 2027 and 0.3% for 2028, November 2026); the growth rate of Media and Insights revenue (Deutsche Bank reported 38.5% growth in Q2 2026); the sustainability of the Core Markets EBITDA margin of 12.5% in Q3 2026, including how much came from manufacturer price increases and stock built ahead of them; general and administrative cost growth after the United States and United Kingdom build-out; depreciation and amortisation of capitalised software (SEK 68.7 million of software investment in the first nine months of 2026, company); recurring items affecting comparability, including United States legal provisions and incentive-related bonuses; net finance costs driven by unhedged revaluation of intercompany loans (SEK 12.5 million in FY2025 and SEK 9.4 million in Q3 2026, company); the effective tax rate (reported -11% for the first nine months of 2026, company) and whether United States and United Kingdom losses create usable tax assets; share dilution from incentive programmes (LTIP 2026/2029, with hurdle prices up to SEK 177.66, and the March 2026 warrant tender); the basis of the brokers' adjusted EPS (FY2025 brokers' basis SEK 4.00 against reported SEK 1.39, FactSet) and what it excludes. Focus: Which of these inputs will decide whether earnings per share recovers as sharply as the sell-side expects over 2027 and 2028, and how far reported earnings per share will stay below the brokers' adjusted figure.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 19:21 to 19:40 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Haypp Group AB (HAYPP-SE): Second set of questions, the operating and financial model (18 months to four years)

Written 2026-11-12 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability of the forecasts; what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before the answers: the model on one page

The frame. Haypp sells nicotine products at a product gross margin of only about 9 to 11% and earns the majority of its gross profit (more than half on the company's statement; 61% on Deutsche Bank's) from Media and Insights, the advertising and data it sells to the same manufacturers that supply it. Using Deutsche Bank's 61%, Q3 2026 Media and Insights gross profit was about SEK 147m against group adjusted EBITDA of SEK 55.5m: without the supplier-paid income the retail operation would lose roughly SEK 90m a quarter. Everything below follows from that. The 2026 to 2030 forecast is a fixed-cost absorption story (G&A built for a much larger business is filled by 13 to 16% revenue growth), financed by a supplier-paid gross profit stream whose gains management deliberately shares with consumers.

My base case against consensus (company basis; SEK m; EPS in SEK, adjusted = before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles).

Full definition of free cash flow: operating cash flow after tax, interest and the cash cost of items affecting comparability, less all capital spending (including capitalised software) and lease principal.

Five points the Consumer must carry into the third and fourth sets.

  1. The 2027 consensus is in substance one broker. Three brokers contribute EPS; Deutsche Bank's 5.80 is the median, Barclays (5.89) gives no revenue or EBITDA, and Pareto is at 3.58. From the consensus EBIT of SEK 198m (the mean of Deutsche Bank and Pareto) and their EPS, I infer that Deutsche Bank sits at about SEK 234m of 2027 EBIT with a normalised 20.6% tax rate, and Pareto at about SEK 162m with about 25%. This is my inference, not a published figure. My SEK 189m sits between them. My 2027 EBIT is only 4% below the consensus EBIT, but my EPS is 29% below the median EPS, because the median is Deutsche Bank's. Roughly SEK 1.0 of the gap comes from EBITDA (mostly revenue), 0.25 from tax, 0.15 from finance cost and 0.1 from share count.
  2. Revenue is where I differ most, and it compounds. My Growth segment grows +34% in 2027 and +30% in 2028; consensus implies +48% and +37.5%. To reach consensus 2027 Growth revenue of SEK 2,165m, the segment must average about SEK 541m a quarter in 2027, against SEK 389m in Q3 2026. That needs sequential growth of about 11% a quarter just as paid marketing is cut and the ZYN base effect ends. The 2026 pace, with heavy marketing, was about 7 to 9% a quarter.
  3. The margin recovery comes mainly from G&A absorption, not from cutting marketing. Measured against the FY2026 average (2.25% of sales), my 2027 marketing (2.2%) barely changes; the Q3 2026 figure of 3.2% is an exit rate, not the annual base. Of my +1.0 percentage point EBITDA margin gain in 2027, about +0.6 comes from G&A leverage (10.7% to 10.1% of sales), +0.3 from gross margin and almost nothing from marketing. Over 2026 to 2030 the +3.8 points of EBITDA margin split into G&A +2.3, gross margin +1.0 and marketing +0.45. I partly disagree with the first set here: the marketing dial is still the largest single swing variable and the downside cushion, but it is not the main source of the base-case improvement.
  4. Free cash flow lags adjusted profit by far more than the first set implied. The first set said FCF turns clearly positive once EBITDA exceeds about SEK 300m. On my figures, 2027 EBITDA of SEK 309m produces only about SEK 49m of full FCF. The deductions are about SEK 108m of capex (of which about SEK 90m is recurring capitalised software), SEK 36m of lease principal, about SEK 50m of cash items affecting comparability (including paying the USD 2.5m provision), SEK 37m of tax, SEK 11m of interest and SEK 18m of working capital. FCF becomes meaningful (about SEK 160m) only in 2028, at about SEK 430m of EBITDA.
  5. 2028 against the targets. At SEK 6.34bn, my 2028 revenue is about 11% below the target floor (18% CAGR from SEK 3.68bn = SEK 7.1bn, at constant currency) and below the first set's SEK 6.6 to 7.0bn. My adjusted EBIT margin of 4.8% sits inside the 4.0 to 7.0% target range, in its lower half, consistent with the first set. My adjusted EBIT of SEK 303m is 2.25 times the 2024 level, against the three- to four-fold ambition (SEK 404 to 538m). So the revenue target fails, the margin target holds at its low end, and the absolute-EBIT ambition fails.

Basis notes (checked against the files; detail in the forecast inputs, Part 4). (a) Company adjusted EBITDA for FY2025 is about SEK 235 to 240m on my derivation: nine months SEK 181.4m, plus Q4 adjusted EBIT of SEK 31.4m (FY SEK 150.7m less nine months SEK 119.3m), plus about SEK 22 to 26m of Q4 depreciation. The brokers'-basis actual of SEK 221m appears to sit after the Q3 2025 San Francisco legal charge. On the company basis, consensus 2026 EBITDA (SEK 220m, which matches the company's nine-month SEK 162.8m plus about SEK 57m in Q4) is therefore a fall of about 7%, not the -0.7% the block's like-for-like line shows. (b) The FactSet consensus "net income" line (-24.8m for 2026, 133m for 2027) appears to be after items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation, while consensus EPS is adjusted; do not divide one by the other. (c) Acquisition (purchase price allocation) amortisation is about SEK 35 to 42m a year (FactSet total D&A of SEK 128m in FY2025 less about SEK 86m in company adjusted EBITDA-to-EBIT depreciation); it sits outside both my and the brokers' adjusted EPS. (d) I derived the Q1 and Q4 segment splits for 2025 and 2026 from group totals, the Q2 and Q3 2026 segment figures, and Deutsche Bank's FY2026 Core margin of 11.1%. They are approximations to within about SEK 20m a quarter.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Bases used throughout. Revenue is the company's reported net sales (FY2025 SEK 3,849m, matching FactSet fundamentals; the brokers'-basis actual is SEK 3,899m). The EBITDA margin is the company's adjusted EBITDA margin: before items affecting comparability, after IFRS 16, so it is before lease costs, and lease principal is entered separately in Part 2. Depreciation and amortisation excludes acquisition (purchase price allocation) amortisation of about SEK 35 to 42m a year, so EBITDA less D&A equals the company's adjusted EBIT. Net financial items include lease interest and assume no currency revaluation (the ±SEK 15m a year noise band is not modelled). The tax rate applies to adjusted pre-tax profit. Working capital is on FactSet's definition (receivables plus inventories less payables: SEK 216m, 5.6% of revenue, at end-2025; SEK 242m, 5.3%, at Q3 2026). Lease payments are lease principal; current lease liabilities were SEK 33.4m at 30 September 2026. The share-count change of 0.50m in 2026 is the March 2026 warrant tender (502,402 new shares issued in exchange for warrants, no cash). If FactSet's 31.8m already includes them, set FY2026 to 0. FY2029 includes conversion of LTIP 2026/2029 hurdle shares.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports two segments, Core Markets (Sweden, Norway) and Growth Markets (US, UK, Germany, Switzerland, Saudi Arabia from July 2026), with segment EBITDA that sums to group adjusted EBITDA (central costs allocated). The FY2025 split and the Q1 and Q4 2026 splits are my derivations from group totals, the reported Q2 and Q3 2026 segment figures (Core EBITDA SEK 87.9m at 10.7% and SEK 104m at 12.5%; Growth -12.4% in Q3) and Deutsche Bank's FY2026 Core margin of 11.1%. They are accurate to about ±SEK 20m a quarter.

Deutsche Bank models a Growth EBITDA margin of -4.5% (2027) and +0.3% (2028), and a Core margin of 11.1% (2026) and 12.1% (2027). My Core revenue is in line. My Growth revenue is 7% below in 2027 and 12% below in 2028. My Growth margin is about one point below Deutsche Bank's in each year.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Core: active consumers +5 to 6% a year (455,000 in Q3 2026); spend per consumer +2 to 3%; the January 2026 Swedish pouch excise (SEK 209/kg) and the 2026 manufacturer list rises lap from January 2027, so Core growth falls from about 16% to 8%. Growth: Q4 2026 is the first quarter compared with a full-ZYN base (ZYN returned on 11 September 2025), so Growth growth falls from +53% organic (Q3) to about +28%. After that, sequential growth of about 7.5% a quarter in 2027 and about 6% in 2028, against about 7 to 9% in 2026 when marketing was heavy. Growth price/mix about -5% in 2027 (manufacturer promotions; ZYN's 20-pouch can cutting revenue per pouch by about 25% until it annualises in Q4 2027), turning flat to slightly positive from 2028. No revenue from capital returns or acquisitions; Saudi Arabia is immaterial to revenue.
  • Gross margin (company): 19.4% (2026; nine months 19.3%), 19.7%, 20.0%, 20.2%, 20.4%. Media and Insights grows about 30% (2027) and 25% (2028), about twice the group rate. About half of the resulting mix gain is reinvested in consumer price (scale economies shared), and the rising Growth mix (lower product margin) offsets part of the rest.
  • Marketing: about 2.25% of sales in 2026 (about 1.5%, 1.8%, 3.2% and 2.4% by quarter). Q4 2026 is the first quarter of reduced lower-funnel spend. 2.2% in 2027 (2.3% in Q1, 2.4% in Q2 before the 1 June 2027 UK advertising ban, 2.1% in H2), then 2.0%, 1.9%, 1.8%. Part of the "cut" is relocated to price, per management.
  • Fulfilment: flat at about 1.8% of sales. Automation gains (cost per can down 6% in Q3 2026; Texas about one-third used; Milton Keynes capacity quadrupled) offset the rising Growth mix.
  • G&A: about SEK 510m (10.7% of sales) in 2026, then +8% in 2027 (SEK 556m, 10.1%) and about +7% a year after (SEK 595m, 637m, 678m; 8.4% of sales by 2030). The US and UK organisational build-out is complete (G&A flat sequentially in Q3 2026). G&A absorption contributes about +2.3 of the +3.8 points of EBITDA margin gain from 2026 to 2030; gross margin about +1.0; marketing about +0.45.
  • Seasonality in Part 3: Q1 is the weakest margin quarter, and Q1 2027 compares with a quarter before most of the 2026 organisation was in place, so it shows little margin gain (4.7% against about 4.8%). Q3 benefits from holiday accruals in G&A. The year-on-year margin recovery shows from Q2 2027.
  • D&A (excluding acquisition amortisation): about SEK 86m (2025) to SEK 110m (2026; nine months SEK 80.7m) on new warehouses, the V3 storefront and leases; then +5 to 9% a year.
  • Tax: above the Swedish 20.6% while the local US and UK entities lose money, without (on my assumption) recognised deferred tax assets. 2024's rate was 23.7% with Growth near breakeven. The rate falls toward 22% as Growth turns profitable. Upside if loss carry-forwards are recognised.
  • Capex: about SEK 90m a year of capitalised software and IT (SEK 68.7m in nine months of 2026) plus SEK 20 to 30m of warehouse equipment. 2026 includes the Q4 roll-out of biometric locker verification in Sweden.
  • Items affecting comparability (not in Parts 1 to 3; below adjusted EBITDA): about SEK 65m booked in 2026, SEK 30m in 2027 (plus paying the USD 2.5m, about SEK 23.5m, provision), SEK 25m in 2028 and SEK 20m a year in 2029 and 2030, all cash. These reduce reported earnings and my FCF cross-check, but not the model's adjusted figures.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

There is no annual guidance. The 2028 targets (capital markets day, April 2025; reiterated at Q3 2026) are constant-currency revenue CAGR of 18 to 25% from 2024 (SEK 3,680m, so SEK 7.1bn to 9.0bn in 2028), an adjusted EBIT margin of 5.5% ±1.5 points, and an ambition of three- to four-fold adjusted EBIT from 2024 (SEK 134.5m, so SEK 404m to 538m). Management also expects G&A intensity to moderate, Growth investment to stay elevated through mid-2027 (Deutsche Bank's reading), and lower-funnel marketing to be reduced from Q4 2026. All free cash flow is reinvested and no dividend is expected.

My inputs against the targets: 2028 revenue SEK 6.34bn, 11% below the floor (before any currency effect), equal to a 14.5% CAGR from 2024. Adjusted EBIT margin 4.8%: inside the range, in its lower half. Adjusted EBIT SEK 303m, 2.25 times 2024: below the ambition. Against the qualitative commentary my inputs agree on direction (G&A leverage, lower marketing, margin trough in 2026) and differ on speed. The previous dated target (SEK 5bn sales in 2025) was missed by 23%, so I do not anchor on the targets. Against consensus (FactSet median; two brokers on revenue and EBITDA, three on EPS): revenue -1.0%, -4.8%, -7.9%, -10.2% (2026 to 2029); EBITDA 0%, -7%, -14%, -18%; adjusted EBIT 2027 -4% against SEK 198m and 2028 -17% against SEK 363m.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Revenue. The company's net sales equal FactSet fundamentals (SEK 3,849m in FY2025). The brokers'-basis actual is SEK 3,899m (+1.3%); the files do not explain the difference. My growth rates are on the company basis. Consensus like-for-like growth (+23.0%, +21.0%, +18.6%) is computed from the SEK 3,899m base.
  2. EBITDA. Company adjusted EBITDA: nine months 2025 SEK 181.4m, nine months 2026 SEK 162.8m, Q3 2026 SEK 55.5m. FY2025 is about SEK 235 to 240m on my derivation (nine months plus Q4 adjusted EBIT of SEK 31.4m plus about SEK 22 to 26m of Q4 depreciation), against a brokers'-basis actual of SEK 221m and FactSet "operating" EBITDA of SEK 237m. FactSet's quarterly operating EBITDA is after the cash part of items affecting comparability: Q3 2025 SEK 35.9m against adjusted SEK 53.0m; Q3 2026 SEK 25.5m against adjusted SEK 55.5m. Consensus 2026 EBITDA of SEK 220m matches the company's adjusted nine months plus about SEK 57m in Q4, so 2026 to 2028 consensus EBITDA appears to be on the company-adjusted basis. On that basis consensus 2026 EBITDA is a fall of about 7%, not the -0.7% the block's like-for-like line shows. FactSet's history "gross profit" and "gross margin" lines (2.8% in FY2025) are not the company's gross margin (about 18.9% in FY2025).
  3. EBIT. Company adjusted EBIT excludes items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation: SEK 150.7m (FY2025), SEK 82.1m (nine months 2026). FactSet's history "EBIT (adjusted)" (SEK 109m, FY2025) is after acquisition amortisation and is not comparable. Consensus EBIT (SEK 198m, 2027) is on the company-adjusted basis (consensus EBITDA SEK 332m less about SEK 134m of D&A excluding acquisition amortisation). Model EBIT = EBITDA less Part 1 D&A = company adjusted EBIT.
  4. EPS. The brokers' adjusted EPS excludes items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation. Deutsche Bank applies a normalised 20.6% tax rate; Pareto appears to use about 25% (my inference from its EPS and the consensus EBIT). The FactSet consensus net income line (SEK -24.8m in 2026, 133m in 2027, 265m in 2028) appears to be after items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation, so it should not be divided by the share count to compare with consensus EPS. My cross-check EPS is on the brokers' adjusted basis, but at my tax rates.
  5. From my adjusted to reported. Reported pre-tax profit = adjusted pre-tax profit less items affecting comparability (about SEK 65m, 30m, 25m, 20m, 20m) less acquisition amortisation (about SEK 40m, 38m, 38m, 30m, 20m). Reported EPS is about -1.0 (2026), 2.5, 5.3, 8.3 and 11.6, against adjusted 1.88, 4.13, 6.87, 9.54 and 12.62. The reported nine-month 2026 EPS was -1.25 and Q3 2026 -1.39.
  6. Free cash flow. FactSet's free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex only: SEK 160m in 2024, 130m in 2025) omits capitalised software (about SEK 90m a year) and lease payments. My cross-check uses the full definition below.

Definitions and expected differences from the model. Earnings per share is adjusted (before items affecting comparability and acquisition amortisation), computed as (EBITDA + D&A + net financial items) × (1 - tax rate) over average shares (32.2m, 32.4m, 32.5m, 32.8m, 33.1m). It should match the model to within about SEK 0.05 if the model uses year-end shares. Free cash flow is on my full definition: adjusted EBITDA, less the cash cost of items affecting comparability, less cash tax (my tax rate on adjusted pre-tax profit after items affecting comparability), less net financial items, less the change in working capital, capital spending and lease principal.

Expected flags. The model's FCF will be higher than mine by about SEK 40m (2027), SEK 20m (2028) and SEK 15m a year (2029 and 2030), because the model has no line for items affecting comparability. In 2027 the difference also includes paying the USD 2.5m provision. For FY2026 my FCF (SEK 43m) is built from the actual nine-month operating cash flow of SEK 212.8m, which includes a SEK 112.8m working-capital inflow (inventory normalisation, Media and Insights prepayments, accrued but unpaid items), less a partial Q4 reversal of about SEK 50m. A model built on FactSet working capital and currency-inclusive net financial items will show roughly SEK 15 to 20m for 2026. Both differences are real and intended: they are the items that keep Haypp's cash and reported earnings below its adjusted earnings.

Brokers' EPS for comparison: 2.19, 5.80, 9.42, 14.00 (median); Pareto 0.84, 3.58, 8.02; Deutsche Bank 2.19, 5.80, 9.74; Barclays 2.91, 5.89, 9.42.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Haypp Group AB (HAYPP-SE), the Stockholm-listed online retailer of nicotine pouches and snus (storefronts including Snusbolaget, Nicokick, Northerner and Haypp), reporting two segments, Core Markets (Sweden and Norway) and Growth Markets (United States, United Kingdom, Germany, Switzerland and, from July 2026, Saudi Arabia), plus a Media and Insights business that sells retail media, launch services and consumer data to nicotine manufacturers

Request: T-D1

Peers: Chewy (United States online category specialist that moved from heavy losses to profit as marketing intensity fell and sponsored ads grew); Zalando (European online retailer that cut paid marketing and grew retail media and partner services); Maplebear (Instacart) (retail media funded by consumer-goods brands on thin transaction margins); Boozt, Apotea and Lyko (Nordic online retailers that went through investment phases, automation ramps and margin recoveries); Prilla (private United States online nicotine retailer, Haypp's main direct United States rival); Snus Vikings and Two Wombats (private United Kingdom online nicotine retailers dependent on paid acquisition); Philip Morris International, British American Tobacco and Altria (the suppliers whose promotion and channel choices set Haypp's price/mix and fund its Media and Insights income) Operating conditions: a manufacturer promotion war in United States pouches (2025 to 2026); a supplier withdrawing a leading brand from the online channel (the ZYN pause, 2024 to 2025); a sharp reduction in paid customer acquisition after an investment phase; the United Kingdom advertising and sponsorship ban from 1 June 2027; manufacturers cutting trade-promotion budgets as the category consolidates; United States state taxes and flavour restrictions; AI-mediated search changing organic discovery Inputs: Can a Growth Markets segment that reported a -12.4% EBITDA margin in Q3 2026 (company) reach about breakeven by 2028 (Deutsche Bank models -4.5% for 2027 and +0.3% for 2028, 6 November 2026) while revenue keeps growing after the reduction in lower-funnel paid marketing announced on the Q3 2026 call? What did comparable online specialists' customer growth and revenue growth do in the two to four quarters after they cut paid acquisition from an elevated level, and how often did management reverse the cut? Is the Core Markets precedent (EBITDA margin 8.6% in Q2 2024 to 12.5% in Q3 2026, company) transferable to the United States, where users consume about 3.6 to 4 pouches a day against 10 to 12 in Scandinavia (company, Jefferies, July 2026)? How plausible is continued growth of Media and Insights at about twice the rate of group revenue (company statement, Q4 2025 call) when it already supplies more than half of group gross profit (company; Deutsche Bank says 61%, November 2026), and how have peers' retail-media businesses behaved when a few large advertisers cut spending? Can general and administrative costs, which fell to 10.4% of net sales in Q3 2026 from 11.3% in Q2 (company), keep growing well below revenue now that the United States and United Kingdom organisations are built? What does Haypp's own record say: the SEK 5 billion 2025 sales target (actual SEK 3.85 billion), profit shortfalls against consensus reported by brokers for Q2 2025, Q4 2025 and Q3 2026 (DNB Carnegie; Modular Finance consensus compiled by the company), and on-time delivery of the technology and automation programmes? Focus: Whether Haypp can keep Growth Markets revenue growing strongly while cutting paid marketing and moving the segment from heavy losses to breakeven, using strengths it has already shown (organic acquisition, automation, Media and Insights), or whether that needs a commercial discipline it has not yet demonstrated.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Chewy and Zalando (long histories of forecast dispersion through investment and harvest phases); Boozt, Lyko, BHG Group and Apotea (Nordic online retailers whose margins swung widely through campaigns, automation ramps and cost programmes); Maplebear (Instacart) (retail-media growth sensitivity to advertiser budgets); Scandinavian Tobacco Group (lost an online distribution contract in 2025 and saw North America Online and Retail sales fall 13% in Q2 2025); Philip Morris International and British American Tobacco (supplier promotion and channel decisions that move Haypp's volumes and Media and Insights income) Operating conditions: the first quarters of clean comparisons after a supply-shortage base effect (ZYN returned to Haypp in the United States on 11 September 2025); a cut in paid acquisition; supplier budget retrenchment; a supplier restricting online supply; regulatory shocks to remote delivery (Sweden's parcel-locker ruling of October 2026, United States municipal and state enforcement); currency translation with no hedging Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers separating Haypp's next four years from its last six: Growth Markets revenue growth, the Growth Markets EBITDA margin path, Media and Insights growth, general and administrative cost growth, the effective tax rate, and recurring items affecting comparability (SEK 30.4 million in FY2024, SEK 52.6 million in FY2025, SEK 52.7 million in Q3 2026 alone, company)? For online retailers whose profit depended mainly on supplier-funded advertising income, how widely did outcomes disperse around forecasts, and what events widened the range? How often were forecasts met when they rested on one segment turning from losses to profit within two years? How wide was the range of outcomes for online category specialists in the six quarters after a base-effect-driven growth peak? What is the historical dispersion of quarterly profit against consensus for Nordic small-cap online retailers covered by two or three brokers? How much did supplier-driven events (the 2024 to 2025 ZYN pause, which took Growth segment revenue down 26% in Q2 2025, company) move Haypp's revenue and profit, and how quickly did they reverse? Focus: How much of the forecast change rests on the Growth Markets segment and Media and Insights alone, and how wide and how skewed the range of 2027 and H1 2028 outcomes is once supplier and regulatory tails are included.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Boozt, Apotea and Lyko (Nordic online retailers whose recent results show how cost cuts and marketing changes were received against consensus); Chewy (guidance behaviour in a margin-expansion phase); Philip Morris International, British American Tobacco, Altria and Imperial Brands (latest pouch results and promotion commentary as read-across to Haypp's Growth price/mix and Media and Insights); Scandinavian Tobacco Group (Nordic nicotine peer reporting the same quarters) Operating conditions: the Q4 2026 report on 10 February 2027, the first quarter against a full-ZYN base and the first with reduced lower-funnel marketing; Q1 to Q4 2027 and Q1 2028 reports; the United Kingdom advertising ban from 1 June 2027; annual manufacturer contract negotiations for 2027; a possible capital markets day; thin coverage (Deutsche Bank Buy SEK 220, Barclays Overweight SEK 210, Pareto Securities Hold SEK 160 on FactSet, November 2026; DNB Carnegie discontinued coverage on 26 June 2026) Inputs: How likely are Haypp's 2028 targets (constant-currency revenue CAGR of 18 to 25% from 2024; adjusted EBIT margin of 5.5% plus or minus 1.5 points; three- to four-fold adjusted EBIT from 2024, set April 2025 and reiterated at Q3 2026) to be reiterated, re-dated or replaced in the next 18 months? What does the company's record say about re-dating rather than upgrading or cutting targets (the SEK 5 billion 2025 target set before the November 2023 capital markets day)? Where do the brokers' FY2027 and FY2028 forecasts sit against the run-rate implied by Q3 2026 (Growth Markets revenue, segment EBITDA, marketing at 3.2% of sales)? What do the brokers assume for the effective tax rate (Deutsche Bank uses 20.6%), for items affecting comparability and for Media and Insights growth? What did the company-compiled Modular Finance consensus expect before each of the last six reports, and how did results compare? FY2027 consensus EPS fell 53% over twelve months while the target price rose 25% over six months (FactSet, November 2026): what has driven this divergence, and what would make revisions turn positive or negative after the Q4 2026 report? How do peers' latest results on pouch promotions, can sizes (ZYN's move to 20-pouch cans in Q4 2026) and trade-marketing budgets read across to Haypp's next four quarters? Focus: Whether consensus for FY2027 and FY2028 (FactSet: two brokers on revenue and EBITDA, three on EPS) is more likely to be cut or raised after the Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 reports, given the ZYN base effect and the marketing reduction, and whether the 2028 targets survive to the next capital markets day.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Philip Morris International, British American Tobacco, Altria and Imperial Brands (the pouch narrative that dominates sector commentary); Scandinavian Tobacco Group (Nordic nicotine peer); Boozt, Apotea and Lyko (Nordic online retail narrative); Chewy and Maplebear (Instacart) (the retail-media narrative investors apply to online specialists) Operating conditions: a risk-off market regime that penalises small, illiquid growth stocks; exclusion of tobacco-related shares by many Nordic funds; thin sell-side coverage after DNB Carnegie's exit (June 2026); investor-relations focus on United States small-cap conferences (Lytham Partners, Planet MicroCap); the European Commission's tobacco products proposal expected in 2027; the United Kingdom advertising ban from 1 June 2027 Inputs: the slowdown in reported growth expected from Q4 2026 as the September 2025 ZYN return laps, and whether investors read it as a base effect or as weaker demand; whether the Q4 2026 marketing reduction is an elimination or a reallocation into consumer prices; the dependence of group profit on Media and Insights income paid by Haypp's own suppliers; manufacturers' plans to cut trade-promotion spending (Advantage Solutions survey, October 2026) and what that means for Media and Insights; the effective tax rate while United States and United Kingdom entities lose money; the recurrence of items affecting comparability, including cash bonuses that fund executives' incentive-plan participation; capitalised software and the gap between adjusted earnings and free cash flow; the path of the Growth Markets EBITDA margin; ZYN's 20-pouch can and revenue per pouch; AI-mediated search and Haypp's 97% organic acquisition; supplier power to restrict online supply; the possibility of future capital returns once Growth Markets is self-funding. Cover sell-side notes (Deutsche Bank, Barclays, Pareto Securities, Jefferies' non-rated work, past DNB Carnegie notes) and investor commentary on X (Twitter), Swedish investor forums and media (Placera, Avanza, Dagens industri, Börsvärlden, Affärsvärlden), Nordic small-cap newsletters, and United States microcap commentary. Focus: Which of these dynamics investors already discuss and which almost no one does, in particular the base effect, the reallocation character of the marketing cut, Media and Insights concentration, tax and cash conversion, so that it is clear what a disappointment or a surprise in 2027 would be measured against.
